\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning}\\
\midrule
$\mathcal{L}^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F};\mathbb{R})$ & Set of $\mathcal{F}$-measurable real-valued maps, that is set of real-valued random variables.\\
$\mathcal{L}^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu;\mathbb{R})$, $p\in[1,\infty)$ & Set of $\mathcal{F}$-measurable real-valued maps $f$ such that $\lVert f\rVert_{L^p}^p=\int_{\Omega}|f|^p\diff \mu<\infty$ is $\mathbb{P}$-integrable.\\
$\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu;\mathbb{R})$ & Set of $\mathcal{F}$-measurable real-valued maps $f$ such that $\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}=\underset{\mathbb{P}}{\operatorname*{ess\sup}}|f|<\infty$ is $\mathbb{P}$-integrable.\\
$L^p(\Omega, \mathcal{F},\mu;\mathbb{R})$, $p\in [0,\infty]$ & quotient space of $\mathcal{L}^p$ with respect to the equivalence relation $f\sim g\iff \lVert f-g\rVert_{L^p}=0$.\\
$C(V;W)$ & Continuous maps from $V$ to $W$ where $V,W$ denote topological spaces.\\
$\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_i)_{i\in I}$ satisfies the usual assumptions & $\mathbb{F}$ is right-continuous i.e. $\cap_{\epsilon>0}\mathcal{F}_{t+\epsilon}=\mathcal{F}_t$ and is $\mu$-complete.\\
$\mathcal{F}_I=\bigvee_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_i$ & In general $\mathcal{F}_i\subset\mathcal{F}$ and $\mathcal{F}_I\subset \mathcal{F}$ for some index set $I$. We often assume, but do so explicitly, $\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_I$.\\
$\mathcal{B}(E)$&Denotes the Borel-\(\sigma\)-algebra generated by the set of open sets of the topological space \(E\).
\\
$\mathcal{P}$& predictable \(\sigma\)-field, that is the \(\sigma\)-field generated by adapted, left-continuous processes
\\
$X_*\mathbb{P}(A)$ & Push-forward measure of $\mathbb{P}$ under the measurable map $X:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}$. It is defined as $\mathbb{P}(X^{-1}(A))$ for all $A\in\mathcal{F}$.\\
$\mathbb{T}_+$ & $\mathbb{R}_+\cap[0,T]$ or $\mathbb{N}_+\cap[0,T]$ depending on context: continuous/discrete.\\
$(\tau,\sigma]$ & For $\tau,\sigma$ random times, this denotes the stochastic interval $\{(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times \mathbb{T}_+:\tau(\omega)<t\leq\sigma(\omega)\}$.\\
$v^{tr}$, $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$ & Transpose of the $n$-tuple $v$.\\
$\left[n\right]$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$ & $\{1,\dots,n\}$.\\
$X^*$ & for $X$ a topological vector space, if not stated otherwise, $X^*$: topological dual of $X$.\\
\text{càdlàg} & right-continuous with left limits.\\
\text{càglàd} & left-continuous with right limits.\\
$b\mathcal{M}$ & for a set of real-valued maps $\mathcal{M}$, $b\mathcal{M}$ denotes the set of bounded maps in $\mathcal{M}$.\\
$\Delta X_t$ & $X_t-X_{t-}$, where $X_{t-}=\lim_{s\to t,\,s<t}X_s$.\\
$X:(\Omega,\mathcal{F})\to(E,\mathcal{E}) \text{ is } \mathcal{F}/\mathcal{E}$-measurable & For every \(A\in \mathcal{E}\) we have \(X^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}\). If \(X\) is real-valued and nothing else is mentioned, we take \(\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\) and simply say that \(X\) is  \(\mathcal{F}\)-measurable. 
\\

\chapter{Introduction}
\label{chap:introduction}

\section{Motivation and context}
\label{sec:introduction-motivation-context}
One of the central problems addressed in mathematical finance is the pricing and hedging of contingent claims. This is best addressed in the framework of probability theory, formalizing the flow of information available in the market and the random evolutions of underlying prices. In probability theoretic jargon, the adapted notions are filtered probability spaces and random variables. The flow of information is then modeled by the filtration, that is, the non-decreasing sequence of set
collections \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) with a fixed terminal time \(T\in(0,\infty]\), where the information available to the seller/buyer at time \(t\) is given by the sets contained in the collection \(\mathcal{F}_t\). The market consists of a price process \(X\) representing the prices of some randomly evolving risky assets, some non-risky assets, e.g. bank accounts, growing at a fixed interest rate, and agents trading in the market by holding or selling dynamic portfolios \((\eta_t,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) where \(\eta_t\) denotes the number of units of shares of the risk-less asset held at time \(t\) and \(\vartheta_t\) the number of units of shares of risky assets held at time \(t\). 
Mathematically, such an ordered collection of random variables is called a \textit{stochastic process}. There are multiple ways to look at a stochastic process \(Z\).
\begin{itemize}
    \item a real-valued map \(Z:(\omega,t)\mapsto Z_t(\omega)\)
    \item a collection of random variables \(Z=(Z_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\), \(Z_t:\omega\mapsto Z_t(\omega)\)
    \item a path-valued random variable \(Z(\omega)=\left(t\mapsto Z_t(\omega)\right)\).
\end{itemize}Mathematically speaking, hedging denotes the problem of achieving a random payoff \(H\) with a dynamic portfolio  \((\eta_t,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) at time \(T\), without injecting or extracting money along the way. \footnote{Otherwise, the problem is trivial, given an unlimited reserve of cash.} Such \textit{self-financing} portfolios are completely determined by \((\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) and the initial wealth \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\). Denoting by \(G_t(\vartheta)\) the cumulative gains and losses of holding the strategy \((\eta_t,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\)  up to time \(t\) we can write down the value of a self-financing strategy at time \(t\) as follows:
\begin{equation}
    V_t=V_0+G_t(\vartheta)\;.
\end{equation}
The problem of hedging thus takes on the form 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first notion of heding}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}}m(H-V_T(\vartheta))\;,
\end{equation}
where \(m\) denotes the choice of how one measures and weights the error committed by hedging. 
Over what set of portfolios the minimization should be considered, and whether the infimum is achieved or not are the central questions one would like to answer in a mathematically precise way. In particular, they will depend on our choice of the measure \(m\). This article predominantly treats \textit{mean-variance} hedging, which chooses for \(m\) a quadratic criterion. The corresponding value to be minimized is then
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem}
    \mathbb{E}\left[
        \left(H - V_0 - G_T(\vartheta)\right)^2
    \right]\;.
\end{equation}
In this case, we shall see that the main approach to solving \cref{eq:first notion of heding} is to study how to reduce \cref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} to a projection problem in \(L^2\). \(L^2\) being a inner product space, admits an inherent notion of orthogonality. Finding the best approximation of \(H\) by a self-financing strategy in \(L^2\) then reduces to orthogonally decomposing \(H\) into a part in the subspace generated by \(V_T(\vartheta)\), ranging over the strategies \(\vartheta\) we want to admit, and a part orthogonal to it. In order for this approach to make any sense, we require \(H\in L^2\) and restrict the set of strategies we allow to \(\Theta\) such that the terminal wealth \(V_T\) is in \(L^2\). Explicitly, considering the following set
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{A}(\Theta)=\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta) \;,
\end{equation}
the question of the existence of a solution to \cref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} reduces to the question of whether \( \mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) is a closed subspace in \(L^2\). 
\bigbreak
More generally, we can always find a sequence of portfolios \((V_0,\vartheta)\) such that the corresponding hedging errors converge to the optimal one. We might thus ask whether we can find an explicit approximation sequence? If the objects were deterministic functions, we might look for candidates of dense systems of functions. A particular choice is to use neural networks and their approximative power. For a stochastic process, we might try to approximate its random paths. However, this is not a good approach, as in order to approximate a random path, one needs to know how the path looks beforehand. In the time discrete case, a clever approach is to approximate the "deterministic" part of the random variable \(V_t\) for every \(t\). For this, we will use some structure of the filtration, which we assume to be generated by some process \(Y\) representing the information available in the market. The key insight is to understand that, due to the Doob-Dynkin representation, any random quantity whose value at time \(t\) can be inferred without error, knowing the state of the market at that same time, can be written as the composition of a deterministic function and the randomness from the market itself. Mathematically speaking, this broadly states that any \(\mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(Y_k:0,\dots,n)\)-measurable random variable \(\vartheta_k\) can be written as
\begin{equation}
    \vartheta_k = f(Y_0,\dots,Y_k)\;,
\end{equation}
for some, deterministic \(f\).
The key insight is that by approximating the deterministic function \(f\), we approximate the random variable \(\vartheta_k\). If we do so for any point in time, we can thus approximate the stochastic process by neural networks in this sense. This is the insight in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. Nevertheless, it is not straightforward how this is supposed to generalize to the time-continuous case. We will see that by naively approaching this generalization, we run into multiple technical problems, hence motivating the approach taken in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}. 
\bigbreak
The combination of this random neural approximation and the study of the mean-variance hedging problem yields an approximation sequence of neural strategies approximating the optimal mean-variance solution. This leads to the continuous time mean-variance hedging result \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025} formulated in the case where the price process \(X\) is a square integrable martingale. The adapted notion of admissible strategies \(\Theta\) is then given by the space \(L^2(M)\), which can be identified as an actual \(L^2\)-space. In this case, the respective theory of stochastic integration turns out to be very robust, in the sense that \(\mathcal{A}(L^2(M))\) is naturally a closed \(L^2\) subspace. The case where \(X\) is a special
semimartingale with a canonical decomposition.
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:introduction-canonical-decomposition}
    X = X_0 + M + A\;,
\end{equation}
then leads to the natural generalization of \(L^2(M)\). 
The appropriate space of square-integrable strategies is therefore of the form
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:introduction-strategy-space}
    \Theta = L^2(M) \cap L^2(A)\;.
\end{equation}
The same question arises again. Given the choice \(\Theta=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\), does a solution to \cref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} exist, and can we approximate it with some appropriate network related strategies? The aim of this thesis is, first of all, to precisely formulate this questions and their respective context in order to answer them in a satisfactory way. 

\section{Scope and contribution}
\label{sec:introduction-scope-contribution}

The purpose of this thesis is purely theoretical. We do not study numerical implementations,
training algorithms, simulation procedures, or empirical performance. Instead, we investigate
the mathematical approximation problem underlying a continuous-time version of deep
mean-variance hedging. The role of neural networks in this thesis is therefore not primarily
computational, but functional-analytic: they provide approximation classes for the
deterministic functions from which predictable trading strategies are generated.

Several parts of the thesis are expository. We recall the necessary background on hedging,
convex risk measures, mean-variance hedging, neural network approximation, and stochastic
integration. This material is included to make the later continuous time argument
self-contained and to clarify the relation between deep hedging and classical
mean-variance hedging, as well as the core insights involved in generalizing the discrete time setup of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} to the continuous time analog presented in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}.

The main contributions of the thesis are the following. First, we make explicit how the deep
hedging framework of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} can be specialized to the quadratic loss and hence to a
finite discrete-time mean-variance hedging problem. We spell out this specialization and connect it to the classical
mean-variance formulation.

Secondly, we give the context to \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} approach in presenting the relevant density results for neural networks and mean-variance hedging in the \(\mathcal{H}^2/L^2(M)\)-framework. 

Thirdly, we argue why the approach taken in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} is particularly elegant and why a more direct approach fails. 

Finally, we show how the continuous-time mean variance hedging result of \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} can be generalized to certain semimartingale price processes. We set our approach into the context of the study of the mean-variance problem in the semimartingale case in \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}.

\section{Outline of the thesis}
\label{sec:introduction-outline}

\cref{ch:hedging} introduces the basic mathematical setup used throughout the thesis. We introduce and motivate the stochastic basis, discounted price processes,
predictable trading strategies, self-financing portfolios, trading gains, and the space of
terminal values generated by admissible strategies. We then discuss two hedging criteria:
convex risk measures and the quadratic criterion leading to mean-square hedging.

\cref{ch:Deep Hedging in Discrete Time} reviews the finite discrete-time deep hedging
framework of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. We explain how predictability and the Doob-Dynkin lemma
reduce the approximation of trading strategies to the approximation of deterministic
functions of market information. We then specialize the framework to the quadratic loss
and formulate the corresponding finite discrete-time deep mean-variance hedging result.

\cref{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation} recalls the neural network approximation results used
later. We focus on shallow feedforward neural networks and their density properties in
spaces of continuous functions and \(L^p\)-spaces. These results provide the deterministic
approximation tools needed to construct algorithmically generated trading strategies.

\cref{ch:continuous time mean variance hedging} develops the continuous-time
mean-variance hedging framework. We first treat stochastic integration with respect to
square-integrable martingales and discuss the corresponding Hilbert space structure. We then
pass to special semimartingales. This chapter also recalls the closedness problem for
\(G_T(\Theta)\), which is central to mean-variance hedging in the semimartingale case.

\cref{ch:Approximation by Algorithmic Strategies in Continuous Time} studies the approximation of
predictable strategies by algorithmically generated strategies in continuous time. We explain
why a naive extension of the finite discrete-time argument is insufficient and discuss the relevant notion of convergence. We then construct suitable
classes of algorithmically generated predictable processes, prove corresponding density statements, and infer the resulting approximation theoretic results. 

\cref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time} combines the approximation
results with the mean-variance hedging problem. We first formulate the resulting deep
mean-variance hedging statement in the martingale case. We then extend the argument to
suitable semimartingale price processes by using the \(L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\)-framework and
the closedness properties of spaces of stochastic integrals.





\chapter{Mathematical Setup of Hedging}\label{ch:hedging}
In order to explain the concept of hedging, we first convey a conceptual understanding. This will motivate the probabilistic set-up that leads to a well defined mathematical problem, whose solution may be studied analytically.
\\\\
We formulate hedging from the perspective of a seller of a European call option. The latter gives the buyer the option to buy the underlying asset at the time of maturity at the strike price \(K\). the strike price is known at the time the option was acquired. Crucially, the owner of the option can only exercise the contract at maturity, not before. At maturity, the owner of the option will exercise his right to buy if the price of the underlying asset \(S\) at maturity is higher than the strike price \(K\). If the price of the underlying asset at maturity is below the strike price, the owner of the option doesn't exercise his right to buy. Thus, the payoff of the option for the owner is given by 
\begin{align}
    H = \max(S_T-K,0)\;.
\end{align}
Here \(T<\infty\) is the time of maturity.
\\\\
Note that the price \(S\) at the maturity of the underlying asset is modeled to be stochastic, whereas the strike price is deterministic. Hence, for a given state of the world \(\omega\), the random payoff of the European call option is \(H(\omega)=\max(S_T(\omega)-K,0)\).
\bigbreak
From the perspective of the seller of the European call option, the gain of the buyer of the European call option is the amount of money he is obliged to pay. We call this a \textit{liability}. Intuitively, the seller will ask for a price for the option equal to the expected payoff of the option. In order for the seller to be able to pay out the payoff of the call option, he might be interested in investing in the stock market himself and hoping to make at least a profit that enables him to compensate for his liability. This is what we call \textit{Hedging}. The questions arising are hence: Is there a trading strategy that will give me, almost surely, a gain at least equal to the payoff of the liability? If not, how close can I get? What does it mean to be close? What is the right price I should ask for the option? In order to give satisfactory answers, we will set up the mathematical basis allowing for a precise formulation of these questions and an analytic treatment. 
\section{Self-financing strategies and trading gains}
The results presented in this article will be formulated on finite and infinite time horizons. To accommodate both cases elegantly, we introduce the following notation
: Let \([0,T]\subset[0,\infty]\). For discrete time models, we take \([0,T]\subset\overline{\mathbb{N}}\). We call \(T\) the final time. We may have \(T<\infty\) and/or \(T=\infty\) if it makes sense. We will be explicite about this choice at the beginning of every section. For \(\mathbb{R}_+=[0,\infty)\) we adopt the following notation 
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{R}_+\cap [0,T]
\end{align}
In the case where \(T<\infty\) we have \(\mathbb{T}_+=[0,T]\). In the case where \(T=\infty\) we have \(\mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{R}_+\). The discrete case is denoted in the same way and is understood from context which wether \(\mathbb{T}_+\) is a subset of \(\mathbb{R}_+\) or \(\mathbb{N}\).
\bigbreak
We assume an underlying probability space 
\begin{equation}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\;.
\end{equation}
Furthermore, we shall assume that the probability space is \(\mathbb{P}\)-complete, that is the \(\sigma\)-algebra \(\mathcal{F}\) contains 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nullsets}
    \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}=\left\{A\in2^{\Omega}:\exists B\in\mathcal{F}, A\subset B, \mathbb{P}(B)=0\right\}\;.
\end{equation} 
To model the flow of information available to agents in the market we further introduce a filtration  \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in \mathbb{T}_+}\), where
\(\mathcal{F}_t\subset\mathcal{F}\) is non-decreasing in \(t\) as a sequence of sub-\(\sigma\) fields.\\\\
Moreover we require that the available information is generated by  \(\mathbb{R}^{r}\)-valued random process \(Y=(Y_t)_{t\in \mathbb{T}_+}\), where \(r\) is a natural number. This means that we set 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filtration generated by market information process}
    \mathcal{F}_t=\sigma(Y_s:0\leq s\leq t)
\end{equation}
We refer to \(Y\) as the \textit{market information process} and denote the corresponding filtration by \(\mathbb{F}\) and refer to it as the \textit{raw} filtration.
We refer to \(Y\) as the \textit{market information process}. Defining 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{F}_T=\bigvee_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\mathcal{F}_t\;,
\end{equation}
we take the standard assumption 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{F}_T=\mathcal{F}\;.
\end{equation}
We want the filtration to be \(\mathbb{P}\)-complete as well, that is \(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subset\mathcal{F}_0\). Note that the filtration \cref{eq:filtration generated by market information process} is not necessarily complete.
Thus we take instead
\begin{equation}
    \overline{\mathcal{F}}_t=\sigma(Y_s:0\leq s\leq t)\vee \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\;.
\end{equation}
We denote by the corresponding filtration by \(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\).
Hence, the stochastic basis is 
\begin{equation}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\overline{\mathbb{F}},\mathbb{P})\;.
\end{equation}

 We give a useful charaterisation of the completed \(\sigma\)-algebra as stated in \parencite[Thm.~1.9]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:characterisation of completed sigma-algebra}
    Let \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\). Then we have that 
    \(\mathcal{C}=\left\{A\cup E:A\in\mathcal{F},E\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\right\}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra and equal to \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The fact that \(\mathcal{C}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra can be found in \parencite[Thm.~1.9]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}. The equality follows from \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma(\mathcal{C})\) by definition and the fact that \(\mathcal{C}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra.
\end{proof}
The price of \(d+1\) tradable assets will be modeled by a \(\mathbb{R}_+^{d+1}\)-valued random process \(S = (S_t)_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\), with \(S_t(\omega) = (S_t^0(\omega),\dots,S_t^d(\omega))\in \mathbb{R}_+^{d+1}\) for every \(t\in \mathbb{T}_+\). We call \(S\) the price process. Naturally, the stochastic process \(S\) is assumed to be \(\mathbb{F}\)-adapted, i.e., the random variable \(S_t\) is \(\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable. In other words, the price of the assets modeled by \(S\) at time \(t\) is supposed to be known at time \(t\) (and not in advance). We interpret \(S^0\) as the bank account or any other risk free asset. To fix the units of the asset prices once and for all, we define \(X_t^{i}=S_t^{i}/S_t^0\). Hence, \(X_t^0 = 1\) for all times, and we call \(X\) the \textit{discounted} price process. We will still refer to the discounted price process \(X\) as the price process, as we shall always refer to the discounted version. Thus, there is no risk of confusion.
\\\\
We may want to trade in these \(d+1\) assets by holding a certain number \(\vartheta_t\) of risky assets \(X_t^i,\;i=1,\dots,d\) and a number \(\eta\) of risk-free assets \(X_t^0\equiv 1\). We refer to \(\vartheta\) as the \textit{trading strategy} and call the process \((\vartheta,\eta)\) a \textit{portfolio}. It can be adjusted dynamically and generally evolves stochastically over time. We don't want to allow insider trading, i.e., knowing the price at time \(f\) of a risky asset before time \(t\), hence giving the possibility to adjust the prices in a way that makes a profit with probability 1. In order to enforce this requirement, we require strategies to \textit{be predictable} or \(\mathbb{F}\)-\textit{predictable}; that is, we require that the value of \(\vartheta_t\) is known just before time \(t\) (the trader cannot look at what happens at time \(t\) in the market and decide accordingly). 
The discounted \textit{wealth process} is then defined as 
\begin{equation}
    V_t = \vartheta^{tr}X_t+\eta_t1\;.
\end{equation}
By trading the assets \(X\), we denote the accumulated gains and losses up to time \(t\), amounting to \(G_t(\vartheta) = (\vartheta \bullet X)_t\). 
\\ In the case \(\mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{N}\cap [0,T]\) we have: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete accumulated gains}
    (\vartheta \bullet X)_t=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\vartheta_k(X_{k}-X_{k-1})
\end{equation}
and for \(\mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{R}_+\cap [0,T]\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:continuous accumulated gains}
    (\vartheta \bullet X)_t=\int_0^t\vartheta_s\diff X_s\;,
\end{equation} 
where the right hand side denotes the stochastic integral of \(\vartheta\) with respect to \(X\). While \cref{eq:discrete accumulated gains} needs no further justification, we refer to \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} for an extensive disccusion of the construction and properties of the stochastic integral \cref{eq:continuous accumulated gains} in full generality. For a more pragmatic introduction we also refer to \cref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square integrable Martingales} and \cref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square integrable Martingales}. We refer to both objects as stochastic integrals. 
\\\\
We denote by \(L(X)\) the set of \(X\)-integrable processes \(\vartheta\), that is the set, depending on \(X\), of \(\vartheta\) for which \(\vartheta\bullet X\) is well-defined. As stated this depends on properties of the integrator \(X\) and will be made explicite in the respective cases through-out this article. 
\bigbreak
Up to this point, we allow for external cash flow or costs. The cumulative amount of cash injected or taken out is given by the cumulative cost process 
\begin{equation}
    C_t = V_t - (\vartheta \bullet X)_t\;,\quad t\in\mathbb{T}_+\;. 
\end{equation}
Requiring that there is no external cash flow for \(t\in I\) makes the portfolio \textit{self-financed} and the cumulative cost process constant, equal to the initial value of the  portfolio \(C_t=V_0\quad\forall t\in I\). Hence, for a self-financing strategy, the value process \(V\) takes the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:self financing strategy}
    V_t(\vartheta) = V_0 + (\vartheta\bullet X)_t=V_0+G_t(\vartheta)\;,\quad t\in\mathbb{T}_+\;. 
\end{equation}
Notice that \cref{eq:self financing strategy} is fully determined by \(V_0\) and the process \(\vartheta\). Indeed, \(\vartheta\) and \(V_0\) determine \(V_t\) by  \cref{eq:self financing strategy}. Finally, \(\eta\) is then determined by \(V_t = \vartheta^TX_t+\eta_t\). Hence, we denote a self-financing strategy by \((V_0,\vartheta)\).
\\\\
Let \(\Theta\) be a subset of predictable, real-valued stochastic processes. We say that a strategy \(\vartheta\) is \textit{admissible} if \(\vartheta\in\Theta\). The exact definition of \(\Theta\) depends on the context it is used in and will be made explicite in any case.
We define the following set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:terminal value processes}
    \mathcal{A}^X(\Theta)\coloneqq \mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)=\left\{V_0+(\vartheta \bullet X)_T:(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\right\}\;,
\end{equation}
where \(T\) is the final time and \(G_T(\Theta)=\left\{(\vartheta \bullet X)_T:\vartheta\in \Theta\right\}\).\\
Hence, \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) defines the space of terminal values of portfolios \((V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\). The price process \(X=(X_0,\dots,X_d)\) is fixed throughout all of this article; hence, we abuse notation by omitting the dependency of \cref{eq:terminal value processes}. We write \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\).

\section{General idea of hedging}\label{sec:general idea of hedging}
As explained in the \cref{sec:hedging} we want to hedge a liablity \(H\) which we model to be a real-valued random variable whose value is known at the final time \(T\geq 0\), i.e. \(H\in\mathcal{L}^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\). For an concrete example, one might think of \(H\) as a europian call option and \(T\) the strike time of that option.
\\
We call a contingent claim \textit{attainable} if there exists a self-financing strategy \((\vartheta,\eta)\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:atteinable strategy}
    V_T = H\quad \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}\;.
\end{equation}
A market is \textit{complete} if all contingent claims are attainable. In this article we focue on hedging in incomplete markets. Hence, for a given contingent claim \(H\), there doesn't necessarily exist a self-financing strategy that replicates \(H\). We can approach this in three ways.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Either we insist on \(V_T = H\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}\) and let go of the self-financing assumption for the replicating strategy \((V_0,\vartheta)\). This would entail a non-constant cost process, and the criterion we want to minimize is exactly the cumulative costs of the trading strategy.
    \item Alternatively, we could insist on the self-financing condition and let go of the exact replication condition.
    \item Lastly, we could let go of both assumptions.
\end{itemize}
We will concentrate on the second option; that is, we will insist on the self-financing property but let go of the perfect replication property. 
\\\\
In particular, the corresponding hedging problem is to find \textit{the optimal} self-financing strategy \((V_0,\vartheta)\) that best approximates the contingent claim at time \(T\). The nature of the problem now heavily relies on what we mean by optimal. In general terms we consider hedging as minimization of the hedging error 
\begin{equation}
    V_T-H\;,
\end{equation}
under a generalized \textit{hedging criterion}.
\begin{equation}
    m:\mathcal{L}^0(\mathbb{P,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R}})\to\mathbb{R}\;.
\end{equation}
Then, we define the optimal hedging portfolio by the solution to the following minimization problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general hedging}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Theta}m(V_T(\vartheta)-H)\;,
\end{equation}
and the optimal hedging strategy for fixed initial capital \(V_0\) by 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital}
    \inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}m(G_T(\vartheta)+V_0-H)\;,
\end{equation}
At this point, no finiteness or attainability claims are made or deduced from \cref{eq:general hedging}. If, however, \cref{eq:general hedging} (resp. \cref{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital}) admits a solution \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_T(\Theta)\) (resp. \(V_0^*\)), in the sense that the infimum of \cref{eq:general hedging} is attained in \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) (resp. \(G_T(\Theta)\)), we call it an \textit{optimal trading portfolio} (resp. \textit{optimal trading strategy}). The meaning of \textit{optimal} thus depends on the choice of the hedging criterion \(m\) and the class of admissible strategies \(\Theta\). 
\\\\
In what follows, we will consider two different choices for \(m\) convex risk-measure and \(L^2\)-distance.


\subsection{convex risk measures}
As our first choice, we consider the hedging measure \(m\) to be a \textit{convex risk measure}. As the name suggests, it is supposed to measure the risk of holding an asset position. We demand that it satisfies the following
\begin{definition}[Convex Risk Measure]
    We call \(\rho:\mathcal{L}^0(\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}\)
\end{definition} a \textit{convex risk measure} if it is
\begin{itemize}
    \item Montone decreasing: If \(Z_1\geq Z_2\) then \(\rho(Z_1)\leq\rho(Z_2)\)
    \item Convexity: For \(\alpha\in[0,1]\), we have \(\rho(\alpha Z_1+(1-\alpha)Z_2)\leq \alpha\rho(Z_1)+(1-\alpha)\rho(Z_2)\) for any \(Z_1,Z_2\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) 
    \item Cash-Invariant: \(\rho(Z+c)=\rho(Z)-c \quad \text{for all } c\in\mathbb{R}\)
\end{itemize}
Note that we are not enforcing continuity. Nevertheless, if \(\Omega=\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\}\) is finite, then trivially \(\Omega\cong \mathbb{R}^N\) since any \(X\in\mathcal{L}^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) is determined by the tuple \((X(\omega_1),\dots,X(\omega_N))\). Hence, in the finite case we can view \(\rho\) as a convex function over \(\mathbb{R}^N\), thus continuous \parencite[Thm.~10.1]{Rockafellar1970ConvexAnalysis}.
\\\\
The above conditions can be translated in practically relevant requirements. The monotonicity reflects the fact that a position with a larger payoff has a smaller risk assosciated to it. Convexity formalizes the idea that diversification lowers risk. Cash invariance then models the simple idea that injecting capital lowers the risk of the position by that same amount.
\\\\
Furthermore, we call a position \(X\) \(\rho\)-acceptable if \(\rho(X)\leq 0\), and we call \(X\) to be a \(\rho\)-indifferent portfolio if \(\rho(X) = \rho(0)\); i.e., holding the portfolio \(X\) is equivalent, from the point of view of the convex risk measure \(\rho\), to having no position in the market at all. We call \(\rho\) normalized if \(\rho(0)=0\).
\\\\
\begin{remark}\label{rem:minimal amound of injected cash for acceptable position}
    Notice that by cash invariance \(\rho(X+\rho(X))=0\). In other words, \(\rho(X)\) is the smallest amount of cash that needs to be added to the portfolio \(X\) in order to make it \(\rho\)-acceptable.
\end{remark} 
\bigbreak
We now consider the following minimization problem. Let \(\rho\) be a convex risk measure and \(H\in \mathcal{L}^0(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\) a liability. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:risk measure hedging}
    \pi(H) = \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_T(\Theta)}\rho\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right)\;.\footnotemark
\end{equation}
\footnotetext{Note that in general \(\rho(X)\) is not symmetric, hence it makes a difference wether we define \cref{eq:risk measure hedging} with \(V_T-H\) of \(H-V_T\). By monotonicity and the interpretation that \(\rho\) measures the risk for the owner of the liability, e.g. the seller of the option, we choose the option \(V_T-H\), so that holding a bigger liability increases the risk.}
We interpret \(\pi(X)\) as the hedging error under optimal hedging, or equivalently, recalling the discussion of \(\rho(X+\rho(X))=0\), the minimal amount of cash that makes the position \(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\) \(\rho\)-acceptable. Notice that if \(\Theta\) is a convex set, \(\pi\) itself is again a convex risk measure. Indeed, cash-invariance and monotonicity follow directly from the corresponding properties of \(\rho\). For convexity, notice that 
\begin{align*}
    &\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet H)_T-\alpha H_1-(1-\alpha) H_2-)
    \\&=\inf_{\vartheta_1\in\Theta}\rho(\alpha\left( (V_0+(\vartheta_1\bullet X)_T-H_1\right)+(1-\alpha) \left(V_0+(\vartheta_2\bullet X)_T-H_2\right))
    \\&\leq \alpha\inf_{\vartheta_1\in\Theta}\rho( (V_0+(\vartheta_1\bullet X)_T-H_1)+(1-\alpha)\inf_{\vartheta_2\in\Theta}\rho( (V_01(\vartheta_2\bullet X)_T-H_2)
\end{align*}
Taking \(\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\) of the above inequality yields \(\pi(\alpha H_1+(1-\alpha) H_2)\leq \alpha\pi(H_1)+(1-\alpha)\pi(H_2)\), where we used the convexity of \(\Theta\) in the second step and the convexity of \(\rho\) in the third step.
\begin{remark}\label{rem:ill posed problem}
Notice an important subtlety about \cref{eq:risk measure hedging}. Due to the cash-invariance property of the convex risk measure \(\rho\) we have 
\begin{align*}
    \pi(H) = &\inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_T(\Theta)}\rho\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)\\
    &=\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\rho\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)\\
    &=\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\rho\left(H-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)+V_0=-\infty\;,
\end{align*}
if \(\rho\left(H-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)\) is finite,
 we can take \(V_0\) arbitrarily negative.\footnote{ If \(\rho\left(H-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)=+\infty\) the problem is again ill defined.} In this sense, the problem is ill defined. Hence, either we restrict the set of admissible strategies to those that are lower bounded by some real value \(-b\leq 0\), or we can put additional assumptions on the convex risk measure \(\rho\) (see \parencite{Xu2006RiskMeasurePricingHedging} for a sufficient treatment of this matter). 
\end{remark}
Alternatively, we can take a different point of view and consider a fixed, finite, and deterministic \(V_0^*\in\mathbb{R}\) and analyze the minimization problem 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital}
    \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho\left(H-V_0^*-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)\;.
\end{equation}
This situation can be motivated and related to the realistic scenario where \(V_0^*\) is determined as a result of trading derivatives in the market at competitive prices. The infimum of \cref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital} might still not be finite.  In the case where \(\Omega\) is finite, it is enough to require \(\pi(0)>-\infty\), as shown in \parencite[Cor.~3.5.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, which we recall at this point. 
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Finiteness of inf for finite sample space}
    Assume \(\Omega = \left\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\right\}\) for \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(\pi(0)>-\infty\). Then \(\pi(Z)>-\infty\) for all \(Z\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\)\;.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Indeed, as \(\Omega\) is finite, the se \(\sup_{\omega\in\Omega} X(\omega)\equiv\sup X<\infty\). Hence, using the monotonicity of the convex risk measures \(\pi\) and \(\pi(X)\geq\pi(\sup X)=\pi(0)-\sup X>-\infty\).
\end{proof}
If \(\Omega\) is not necessarily a finite set one has to restrict to bounded cumulative gains 
\begin{equation}
G_T^b(\Theta)=\left\{(\vartheta \bullet X)_T:\vartheta\in \Theta, (\vartheta \bullet X)_T\geq -b\right\}\;,
\end{equation}for some \(b\geq 0\). Imposing further assumptions on the convex risk measure \(\rho\) one can show the existence of the solution 
\begin{equation}
    \pi_b(H) = \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathcal{A}_b(\Theta))}\rho(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T))=\rho(H-V_0^*-(\vartheta^*\bullet X)_T))\;,
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{A}_b(\Theta)=\mathbb{R}+G_T^b(\Theta)\). For the proof and the exact assumptions, see \parencite[Lemma~2.5]{Xu2006RiskMeasurePricingHedging} and  \parencite[Thm~2.6]{Xu2006RiskMeasurePricingHedging}.


\subsection{Mean-square hedging}\label{sec:high level discussion of mean-square hedging}
For the second choice of hedging measure \(d\) we take the squared \(L^2\)-norm that is 
\begin{equation}
    m(X)=\mathbb{E}[|X|^2]\;.
\end{equation}
In order to make \cref{eq:general hedging} well defined for this choice of \(m\) we assume \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) and \(\Theta\subset\Theta^2\), where
\begin{equation}
    \Theta^2=\left\{X\text{-integrable, predictable stochastic process}:\;(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\right\} \;.
\end{equation}
The corresponding hedging problem then takes the form 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean squared hedging}
\inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T)\right)^2]\;.
\end{equation}
In this context, if a solution of the form \(V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet X_T)\in\mathbb{R}+ G_T(\Theta)\) exists, we call \(V_0^*\) the \(\Theta\)-mean-variance price and \(\vartheta^*\) the \(\Theta\)-mean-variance strategy. We might not always make the dependency on \(\Theta\) explicit if it is clear from the context.
\begin{remark}\label{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}
    Observe that \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} doesn't suffer from the same ill posedness issue as \cref{eq:risk measure hedging} as \(V_0\) enters quadratically.
Furthermore, notice that if \(G_T(\Theta)\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2\), then so is \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) and \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} reduces to a \(L^2\) projection problem of the \(L^2\)-random variable \(H\) onto \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\). 
Indeed, then \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} asks for the element in the closed subspace \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) that minimizes the \(L^2\)-distance. Recalling that \((L^2(\mathbb{R)},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2})\) is a Hilbert space, we get existence and uniqueness of the minizer of \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} from the Hilbert projection Theorem \parencite[Thm.~12.3]{Rudin1991FunctionalAnalysis}. 
\end{remark}
Notice that due to the continuity of the map, \footnote{It follows as the composition of continuous functions: \(L^2\)-norm and addition in \(\mathbb{R}\). This uses the general fact that for \(d:X\to\mathbb{R}\) continuous for some topological space \(X\), we have for any subset \(Y\subset X\) that \(\inf d(Y)=\inf d(\overline{Y})\), where the closure is taken with respect to the topology of \(X\) for which \(d\) is continuous. For a short proof, see \cref{Appendix:Infima over closure}}.
\begin{equation}
\begin{cases}
    (\mathcal{A}(\Theta),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2})\to(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|_{\mathbb{R}})\\
    V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\mapsto\lVert H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\rVert_{L^2}\;,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
the minimization problem \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} is equivalent to 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean squared hedging over closure}
\inf_{V_T\in \overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_T\right)^2]\;,
\end{equation}
where the closure \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}=\overline{G_T(\Theta)+\mathbb{R}}\) is taken in \(L^2\). In particular, \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}\) is clearly closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\), and hence, by the application of the Hilbert projection theorem, \cref{eq:mean squared hedging over closure} admits a unique solution as the \(L^2\)-projection on the closed subspace \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}\). Nevertheles this doesn not allow to infer an explicit expression for this solution other than that it is an \(L^2\)-limit of portfolios \((V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\). In section \cref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process} we will see sufficient conditions such that \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}=\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_T(\Theta)}\). Then we can at least infer the mean-variance price, while still not revealing the structure of the mean-variance strategy.  If pricing is the major concern, this might be satisfactory.  \\\\
If, however, we want to infer more explicitly regarding the mean-variance strategy, we will need to study conditions on \(\Theta\) and \(X\) for which \(G_T(\Theta)\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Indeed, if this is the case, by the same Hilbert projection argument, we get the existence of a unique \(V_T^*\in G_T(\Theta)\), which, as an element of \(G_T(\Theta)\), takes the form \(V_T^*=\int_0^T\vartheta^*_s\diff X_s\). We touch upon this question in \cref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time} and \cref{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales}. In this case one might study explicit expressions of the mean-variance portfolio.


\chapter{Deep Hedging in Discrete Time}\label{ch:Deep Hedging in Discrete Time}
After establishing an the mean-variance hedging problem in general terms, we further discuss a particular solution approach introduced by \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. The latter treats the hedging problem in a finite and discrete setup. We refer to the approach developped in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} as deep hedging. \\
The section will be build up as follows. First we heuristically discuss its main ideas before presenting the main theoretical results \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. Although the latter particularly treats convex risk-measure hedging in detail, their approach is more general and extends to mean-variance hedging. In the last part of this section, we work out their approach in the mean-variance hedging setting, theoretically laying the ground for the time-continuous generalization of \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} presented in \cref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables} and \cref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time}. \\  In this section, we shall require that \(T<\infty\). 

\section{The deep hedging framework of Bühler et al.}\label{sec:The deep hedging framework of Bühler et al.}
The main idea in the deep hedging approach is to approximate optimal hedging strategies \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) by a suitable set of approximants. In our context, suitable refers to the property that the approximation should be universal and practical. By universal, we mean that the approximation system is rich enough to approximate any admissible strategy in a strong enough sense arbitrarily well. In particular, the last point refers to a limiting result not referring to the efficiency of the approximation, given a finite amount of computational resources. Neural networks will form a well suited choice to construct the approximation system. Nevertheless, the novelty in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} lies in the general idea of how to get an approximation system for random, admissible strategies \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) from well-known approximation systems of deterministic functions. 
\bigbreak
The basic constraints in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} are in their implementation friendly, discrete time, finite sample space setup. Although very restrictive, this allows the authors of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} to formulate their approach in very general terms, allowing for 
non-constant trading costs. \\
In light of our discussion of the continuous time setup, and in accordance with \cref{sec:general idea of hedging}, we will nevertheless consider a frictionless market, that is, without trading costs. In this case, we will show how the theoretical results from \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} apply to mean-variance hedging in the discrete time, finite sample space setup. This in particular adds to \parencite[Remark.~3.4]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and conceptually links their work to our discussion of deep mean-variance hedging in \cref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time}.
\subsection{Market model and trading strategies}
The current setup follows the general setup described in \cref{sec:hedging} under the following simplyfying assumptions. We assume a finitely many trading times \(0=t_0<t_1<\dots<t_n=T<\infty\), and a finite sample space \(\Omega=\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\}\) such that \(\mathbb{P}(\left\{\omega_i\right\})>0\) for all \(i=1,\dots,N\). Hence, the market-filtration \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_k)_{k=0}^n\), with \(\mathcal{F}_n=\mathcal{F}_T\), generated by the \(\mathbb{R}^r\)-valued market process \(Y=(Y_k)_{k=0}^n\), is also finitely generated, that is 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{F}_k=\sigma(Y_0,\dots,Y_k)\;.
\end{equation} 
The Doob-Dynkin representation lemma \parencite[Lemma.~1.14]{Kallenberg2002FoundationsModernProbability} states in this discrete setup that any observable whose value can be determined with the information available up to time \(t_k\) can be represented as a deterministic function of the knowledge from \(Y\) up to time \(t_k\). Hence, setting the stochastic basis as 
\begin{equation}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F,\mathbb{P}})
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_T\), it is conceptually stated that \(Y\) constitutes all the information that is knowable for an agent in the market. In other words, \(\left\{Y_0,\dots,Y_k\right\}\) is the richest possible feature set.  
\bigbreak
Recall our discussion in \cref{rem:ill posed problem} showcasing that the optimization problem \cref{eq:risk measure hedging} is ill defined in the sense that \(\pi(H)=-\infty\) for all \(H\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) if we don't put additional assumptions on \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) and \(\rho\). Thus, we consider the closely related hedging problem \cref{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital}, which doesn't suffer from this degenerate behavior:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler}
    \pi(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)\;,\footnotemark
\end{equation}\footnotetext{\cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} is clearly, \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} is equivalent to \cref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital} by writing \(\widetilde{L}=L+V_0\) for fixed \(V_0\)}
for \(L\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\). Furthermore, \cref{lemma:Finiteness of inf for finite sample space} still applies to \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} and ensures finiteness if \(\pi(0)>-\infty\). \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} is then the hedging problem that is explicitly addressed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}.
\bigbreak
Recall that due to the discussion in \cref{rem:minimal amound of injected cash for acceptable position} \(\pi(H)\) of  \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} is the minimal additional cash that needs to be added to the traders position in order to make it \(\rho\)-acceptable.


\section{Approximation of trading strategies}\label{sec:Approximation of trading strategies}
Adapting the point of view presented in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, we are not interested in sufficient conditions for the infimum in \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} to be attained, but rather in the consistent approximation of \(\pi(L)\) for any \(H\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) through approximating the optimal trading strategies in \(\Theta\). The underlying idea is to leverage the \(\mathbb{F}\)-predictability of the admissible strategies \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n\) by approximating its respective Doob-Dynkin functional representation of its components with a suitable set of functions. Mathematically speaking, we want to find a suitable dense subset of the function space that encompasses the Doob-Dynkin representations of the \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable \(\vartheta_k\). That is:
\begin{equation}
   \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\;,
\end{equation}
where \(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^d\) is Borel-measurable. For simplicity, we assume \(\vartheta_k\) to take values in \(\mathbb{R}\). The multidimensional case follows analogously. 
\bigbreak
In the current setup, \(\Omega\) is assumed to be finite. Hence we have that 
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\right]=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}f_k(Y_0(\omega),\dots,Y_{k-1}(\omega))<\infty\;,
\end{equation}
is finite as a finite sum of finite terms.
In particular, due to
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})}\left[f_k\right]\;,
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) denotes the law of \((Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\)\footnote{That is, the push-forward measure \(\mathbb{P}_*(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\circ(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})^{-1}\).}, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:f is in L1 for finite Omega}
    f_k\in L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\;.
\end{equation}
More generally, we have
\begin{equation}
    f_k\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\;,\quad p\geq 1\;.
\end{equation}
The stochastic basis is in particular a finte measure space; hence \(L^p\subset L^q\) for \(p\geq q\). Thus, we need an approximation system \(\Psi\) that can approximate functions in \(L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\) arbitrarily well. Technically, we want the following density statement to hold for \(\Psi\).
\begin{equation}
\overline{\Psi}^{L^1}=L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\;.
\end{equation}
As \(L^1(\mathbb{P})\) is a metric space this statement is equivalent to the following
\begin{align*}
\forall f\in L^1(\mathbb{P})\;\forall \epsilon>0\;\exists \psi\in\Psi:\;\lVert f-\psi\rVert_{L^1(\mathbb{P})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}}|f(x)-\psi(x)| \mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})(\diff x)<\epsilon\;.
\end{align*}
The case \(p=1\) is enough for the \(\Omega\)-finite case. It will turn out that for more general sample spaces \(\Omega\) we need density in \(L^p,\; p\geq 1\).
We will only motivate and introduce a concrete incarnation of such a \(\Psi\) at the end of this section and discuss it in \cref{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}. For now, and for the rest of this section, we shall remain general.
\bigbreak
We now return to the original task of approximating \(\pi(L)\) of \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} by approximating the strategies in \(\Theta\) using \(\Psi\). Following \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}  we introduce a stratification on \(\Psi\) as follows. Denote by \((\Psi_M)_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\) a sequence of subsets of \(\Psi\) such that 
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(\Psi_M\subset\Psi_{M+1}\quad\forall M\in\mathbb{N}\)\;,
    \item \(\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Psi_M=\Psi\)\;,
    \item for all \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) there exists a parameter space \(\Sigma_M\subset\mathbb{R}^t\) for some \(t\in\mathbb{N}\) depending on \(M\)\;.
\end{itemize}
The last property leads us to interpret \(\Psi_M\) as a subfamily of \(\Psi\) parameterized by \(\Sigma_M\). For instance, we might think of \(\Psi\) as polynomials of arbitrary degree and \(\Psi_M\) as polynomials of degree less than or equal to \(M\in\mathbb{N}\)\footnote{Note that this example only serves the purpose of providing concrete intuition about the introduction of this stratified family \(\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Psi_M=\Psi\). In particular, polynomials are not generally dense in \(L^p(\Omega,\mathbb{P})\) (They are if \(\Omega\subset \mathbb{R}^s,s\in\mathbb{N}\) is compact)}. \\
As we want to approximate strategies with \(\Psi\) we define the following subset of \(\Theta\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted strategies}
    \Theta_M\coloneqq\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=0}^{n-1}\in\Theta:\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1},\vartheta_{k-1}),\quad F_k\in\Psi_M\}\;.
\end{equation}
Because \(\Psi_M\subset\Psi_{M+1}\) for all \(M\geq1\) we have \(\Theta_M\subset\Theta_{M+1}\).
Furthermore, notice the analogy with the Doob-Dynkin representation of the strategy \(\vartheta\). Again due to the Doob-Dynkin representation theorem, \(\vartheta_{k-1}\) is itself already some measurable function of \((Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\); thus, we can omit \(\vartheta_{k-1}\) dependency in the definition \cref{eq:restricted strategies}. Thus, \(\Theta_M\) is the set of strategies whose components (in time) have a Doob-Dynkin representation in \(\Psi_M\). 
We further denote
\begin{equation}
    \Theta_{\infty}\coloneqq \bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Theta_M
\end{equation}

We can now state the restricted optimization problem. 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted risk measure hedging from buehler}
\pi^M(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}\rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)\;.
\end{equation}
The theorem we will state and prove shows that the restricted hedging errors from \cref{eq:restricted risk measure hedging from buehler} converge to the unrestricted hedging error \cref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} for \(M\to\infty\). This constitutes the core theoretical result of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. 
\begin{theorem}[Consistent Approximation of Hedging Errors]\label{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}
 Let \(\Theta_M\subset \Theta\) be as in \cref{eq:restricted strategies} and \(\pi^M\) as in \cref{eq:restricted risk measure hedging from buehler}. Then, for any \(L\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\),
 \begin{equation}
     \lim_{M\to\infty}\pi^M(H)=\pi(H)\;.
 \end{equation}
\end{theorem}
Before giving the proof, we conceptually argue how we expect the proof to proceed. Recall that the strategy \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^{n}\) is a \(\mathbb{F}\)-predictable process. In particular, \(\vartheta_k\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable with \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}=\sigma(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\). By Doob-Dynkin, for every \(k=1,\dots n\), there exists a \(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}\to\mathbb{R}\) measurable function such that. 
\begin{equation}
    \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\;.
\end{equation}
As we have argued previously in \cref{eq:f is in L1 for finite Omega} we have \(f\in L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\). We can thus approximate it by elements in \(\Psi\). Because \(\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Psi_M=\Psi\), for a given approximation error bound \(\epsilon>0\) we can do so by elements of \(\Psi_M\) for \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough. The hope is then that we can also approximate the hedging error arbitrarily well in the sense that we can get \(\epsilon\)-close with large enough \(M\). To show that this works requires care and will be done in the following proof
\begin{proof}
 First, notice that due to the monotonicity \(\Psi_M\subset\Psi_{M+1}\subset\Psi\) we have \(\Theta_M\subset\Theta_{M+1}\subset\Theta\) hence 
 \begin{equation}
     \pi^M(H)\geq\pi^{M+1}(H)\geq\pi(H)\;. 
 \end{equation}
 Hence, the convergence of \(\pi^M(H)\) to \(\pi(H)\) is from above. Hence, it is enough to show that for any \(\epsilon>0\), there exists \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that 
 \begin{equation}
     \pi^M(H)\leq \pi(H)+\epsilon\;.
 \end{equation}
 Furthermore, by the definition of the infimum over a real set, for every \(\epsilon/2>0\), there exists \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) such that
 \begin{equation}
     \rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)\leq\pi(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\;.
 \end{equation}
 Consider this \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^{n}\). By definition, \(\vartheta_k\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable. So, by the Doob-Dynkin lemma, there exists a measurable \(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^d\) such that 
 \begin{equation}
     \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\;,
 \end{equation}
 for all \(k=0,\dots,n\). As argued in \cref{eq:f is in L1 for finite Omega} \(f_k\in L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\) because \(\Omega\) is finite. Let's denote \(\mu\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\). By the density of \(\Psi\) in \(L^1\), there exists a sequence of functions \((F_k^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset \Psi\) converging to \(f_k\) in \(L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mu)\). In particular, we have for 
 \begin{equation}
     \vartheta_{k}^n\coloneqq F_{k}^n(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\in \Theta_{\infty}
 \end{equation}
 that \(\vartheta_{k}^n\to f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) in \(L^1(\Omega,\mathbb{P})\). Passing to a subsequence and by a diagonal argument, we get \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost sure convergence for all \(k\) simultaneously. Morally speaking, we want to pass the limit \(n\to\infty\) inside \(\rho\). Even if \(\rho\) has sufficient regularity, \(\mathbb{P}\)-a.s. convergence is not enough to conclude. Recall that we assumed, in the current setting, that \(\mathbb{P}(\{\omega\})>0\) for all \(\omega\in\Omega\), hence \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost sure convergence is equivalent to convergence everywhere. Thus
 \begin{equation}
     \lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_k^n(\omega)=\vartheta_k(\omega)\quad\forall\omega\in\Omega\;,k=0,\dots,n\;.\footnotemark
 \end{equation}\footnotetext{Note that the continuity of the stochastic integral is trivial in this finite setting. When trying to naivly generalize this approach to a time-continuous one with general sample space \(\Omega\), this step is highly non-trivial.}
 In particular, 
 \begin{align}
(\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\vartheta^n_k(\omega)\cdot(X_{k}(\omega)-X_{k-1}(\omega))\overset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}(\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega)\;.
\end{align}
 Furthermore, notice that \(\mathcal{L}^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{N}\)\footnote{Any random variable \(Z\) on \(\Omega=\left\{\omega_i\right\}_{i=1}^N\) can be identified bijectively with the tuple \((Z(\omega_1),\dots,Z(\omega_N))\in\mathbb{R}^N\).}. Hence, we can identify \(\rho\) with a convex map \(\tilde{\rho}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}\). In the following, we will not differentiate \(\rho\) and \(\tilde{\rho}\). Notice that \(\rho\) is a convex function on a finite dimensional vector space and, as such, is continuous \parencite[Thm.~10.1]{Rockafellar1970ConvexAnalysis}. Thus, we have
 \begin{equation}
     \lim_{n\to\infty}\rho((\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T-H)=\rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)\;.
\end{equation}
In particular, there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that 
\begin{equation}
    \rho((\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T-H)\leq\rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\pi(H)+\epsilon\;.
\end{equation}
Since for every \(\vartheta^n\) there exists \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough for which \(\vartheta^n\in\Theta_M\). Thus, we have 
\begin{equation}
    \pi^M(H)\leq\pi(H)+\epsilon\;,
\end{equation}
which ends the proof.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
 Observe that we showed in particular that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \pi^{\infty}(H)=\pi(H)\;,
    \end{equation*}
    where 
    \begin{equation*}
    \pi^{\infty}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)\;.
    \end{equation*}
    Indeed we found a sequence \((\vartheta_k^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Theta_{\infty}\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\rho((\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T-H)=\pi(H)\;,
    \end{equation*}
    which implies 
    \begin{equation*}
        \inf_{\Theta_{\infty}}\rho((\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T-H)=\inf_{\Theta}\rho((\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T-H)\;.
    \end{equation*}
    Observe that this does not directly follow from the continuity of the map \(\rho:\mathbb{R}^N\to\mathbb{R}\) by \cref{Appendix:Infima over closure}, because \(\Psi\) is dense in \(L^1\) with respect to the \(L^1\)-topology, which is not the topology for which \(\rho\) is continuous. The argument used in the proof to establish convergence everywhere is crucial. 
\end{remark}
\section{From convex risk measures to quadratic loss}
We show in this subsection that the ideas developed in the proof of \cref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors} can also be applied to the problem \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} in the setup of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. Note that this is not a mere consequence of \cref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors} as the square of the \(L^2\)-norm is not a convex risk measure. For instance, it doesn't satisfy cash-invariance, and the monotonicity property is reversed. The observation that the ideas are applicable to the mean-variance hedging problem is made \parencite[Rem~.3.4.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} for the case where \(V_0\) is fixed exogenously. We show it here explicitly and extend the remark by demonstrating how to treat the joint minimization over \((V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\). 
For fixed \(V_0\) we show a similar statement to  \cref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}. 
We argue that this is a stronger statement than the mean-variance hedging problem \cref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}. 

\subsection{Approximate optimal hedging in the context of deep hedging}
As before, we consider the same setup, that is, a finite sample space \(\Omega\), finite trading times \((t_i)_{i=0}^n\) with \(t_n=T<\infty\). We also take \(\vartheta_k\) real-valued for all \(k=0,\dots,n\) and  neglect trading costs: \(C_t=0\quad \forall t\geq 0\). We study the following mean-variance hedging problem for fixed \(V_0\).
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hedging error}
    \Delta(H) = \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}[\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X\right)_T-H)^2]
\end{equation}
and in analogy to \parencite[Thm.~4.9.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} we introduce
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted mean variance hedging error}
    \Delta^M(H) = \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}\mathbb{E}[\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right)^2]
\end{equation}
for \(\Theta_M\) as in \cref{eq:restricted strategies} and \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\) fixed as in the setup of \cref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital}. We want to show the following discrete time deep mean hedging theorem.
\begin{proposition}[Finite Deep Mean variance Hedging]\label{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}
    \begin{equation}
        \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)^2]=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)^2].
    \end{equation}
\end{proposition}
More precisely, we will show the stronger statement
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}
Let \(\Theta_M\) be as in \cref{eq:restricted strategies}, \(\Delta^M(H)\) as in \cref{eq:restricted mean variance hedging error}, and \(\Delta(H)\) as in \cref{eq:hedging error} respectively. Then, for any \(H\in\mathcal{L}^0(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\),
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta^M(H)=\Delta(H)\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{theorem}
First we show as a lemma that \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} implies \cref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:strong form implies weak form}
    Assume \(\lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta^M(H)=\Delta(H)\).  Then in particular, \(\Delta^{\infty}(H)=\Delta(H)\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let
\[
J(\vartheta):=\mathbb E[(V_0+(\vartheta\cdot X)_T-H)^2].
\]
for fixed \(H\).
Since \(\Theta_M\subseteq \Theta_\infty\subseteq \Theta\), taking infima reverses inclusions, hence
\[
\Delta(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}J(\vartheta)
\leq
\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_\infty}J(\vartheta)
=
\Delta^\infty(H)
\leq
\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}J(\vartheta)
=
\Delta_M(H)
\]
for every \(M\in\mathbb N\). Letting \(M\to\infty\) and using the assumption
\[
\lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta^M(H)=\Delta(H)\;,
\]
we obtain
\[
\Delta(H)\leq \Delta^\infty(H)\leq \Delta(H)\;.
\]
\end{proof}
For the proof of \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} we proceed very similarly to the strategy used in the proof of \cref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}.
\begin{proof}
By definition, we have \(\Theta_{\infty}=\bigcup_{M\geq 1}\Theta_M\subset\Theta\) and \(\Theta_M\subset\Theta_{M+1}\) for all \(M\in\mathbb{N}\). Hence, 
\begin{equation}
    \Delta^M(H)\geq\Delta^{M+1}(H)\geq\Delta(H)\;.
\end{equation} So in particular, it sufficed to show that \(\forall \epsilon>0\;\exists M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(\Delta^M(H)\leq\Delta(H)+\epsilon\).
The proof now is exactly the same as in \cref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors} just using the fact that the expectations
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Continuity of L^2 norm}
    \mathbb{E}[\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right)^2]=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega)-H(\omega)\right)^2\;,
\end{equation}
are finite sums because the sample space \(\Omega = \left\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\right\}\) is finite and the square function is continuous everywhere. As we are dealing with expectations, we don't even need convergence in every \(\omega\in\Omega\) as almost everywhere convergence of the approximation functions \(\vartheta_k^n\coloneqq F_{k,n}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) to \(\vartheta_k\) is enough.
\end{proof}
This shows that, as alluded to in \parencite[Remark~3.4]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, the approximate optimal hedging problem, and in particular the mean-variance hedging problem, is subsumed in the framework of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. Indeed, the proof above for \(l(\cdot)=(\cdot)^2\) goes through for any continuous function \(l:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_+\).
\bigbreak
We remark that in the current setting, \cref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging} can also be proved directly by observing that \(\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert}=\Theta\) and that the map \(\vartheta\mapsto\lVert H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\rVert_{L^2}\) is continuous with respect to the norm induced by \(\lVert\cdot\lVert\). For details, we refer to the appendix: \cref{Appendix:Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}.
\bigbreak
We finish by extending \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} to the joint case \cref{eq:mean squared hedging}, minimizing over the initial capital as well. For this, we use the notation 
\begin{equation}
    J(V_0,\vartheta)\coloneqq \mathbb{E}[(V_0+(\vartheta\cdot X)_T-H)^2]
\end{equation}
and 
\begin{align*}
&\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)=\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}J(V_0,\vartheta)
\\
&\Delta^M_{\mathbb{R}}(H)=\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta_M}J(V_0,\vartheta)
\end{align*}
Furthermore, we write for fixed \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\)
\begin{align}
    &\Delta_{V_0}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}J(V_0,\vartheta)\\
    &\Delta^M_{V_0}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}J(V_0,\vartheta)
\end{align}
\begin{proposition}
    Let \(\Theta,\Theta_{\infty}\) and \(\Theta_M\) be as in \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^M(H)=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)\;.
    \end{equation}
 In particular, we have 
    \begin{equation}
    \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)^2]=\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)^2].
    \end{equation}
    \begin{proof}
        The proof relies on \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}. First, observe that due to 
        \begin{equation}
            \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_M\subset \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{M+1},\;\forall M\geq 1\;,
        \end{equation}
        we 
        \begin{equation}
            \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^M\geq \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^{M+1},\;\forall M\geq 1\;.
        \end{equation}
        Hence, it suffices to show that for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(M\) such that 
        \begin{equation}
           \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^M(H)< \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)+\epsilon\;.
        \end{equation}
        By definition of the infimum, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(V_0^{\epsilon/2}\) such that 
        \begin{equation}
        \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}>\Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)\;.
        \end{equation}
        Fix this \(V_0^{\epsilon/2}\). By \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} we have
        \begin{equation}
            \lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}^M(H)=\Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)\;.
        \end{equation}
        As we observed in \cref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}, the convergence is from above, so equivalently we have that for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\in\mathbb{N}\) big enough such that
        \begin{equation}
            \Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}>\Delta^{M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)
        \end{equation}
Thus, we have
        \begin{align*}
            \Delta^{M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}_{\mathbb{R}}\leq\Delta^{M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)<\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)+\epsilon\;,
        \end{align*}
which shows the first claim. The second part follows exactly as in \cref{lemma:strong form implies weak form} with the trivial observation
        \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_M\subset \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{\infty},\;\forall M\geq 1\;,
        \end{equation}
 Hence, the second part follows from the first part by the same reasoning as in \cref{lemma:strong form implies weak form}.
        \end{proof}

\end{proposition}
\chapter{Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}\label{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}
\section{Feedforward neural networks}
Regarding our discussion in \cref{sec:The deep hedging framework of Bühler et al.} all we required was an approximation system \(\Psi\) to be a set of real-valued functions with the \(L^1\)-approximation property
\begin{equation}
    \overline{\Psi}^{L^1}=L^1(\mathbb{P})\;.
\end{equation}
The reader familiar with the work of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} or by simple guesswork regarding the title of this article will have inferred that we shall take \(\Psi\) to be the set of neural networks. Indeed, the theorems we will discuss in this section show that shallow, feed-forward neural networks fulfill the assumptions for \(\Psi\). It still remains to argue why one would be especially interested in this particular choice. We might argue from a theoretical side as well as empirically from a practitioner's perspective.
From a theoretical perspective, we refer to the arguments provided by \parencite{Bolcskei2018OptimalApproximationSparseDNN}. 
\bigbreak
\textcolor{blue}{Need to argue concisely why neural networks are distinclty usefull.}
\section{Universal approximation}
In this section, we formally introduce shallow, feedforward neural networks and discuss their approximation theoretical properties topologically. In other words, we show that the set of shallow feed-forward neural networks, for an appropriate activation function, forms a dense subset of continuous functions on a compact domain and of \(\mathcal{L}^p\) spaces for any \(p\geq 1\) under a finite measure. We present the proofs given in \parencite{Hornik1991} in detail, as their arguments generalize well to more abstract spaces, and the proof ideas, as we shall see at the end of this section. Take any occuring measure, positive or signed, to be finite as a standing assumption. 


\subsection{Density of Shallow Feed-Forward Neural Networks}
The main interest of this section is to approximate functions of \(k\) real variables by finite linear combinations of the form 
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{j=1}^Nc_j\psi(A_j(\cdot))\;,
\end{equation}
where \(A_j(x)=w_j^{tr}x+b_j\) is an affine linear map from \(\mathbb{R}^k\) to \(\mathbb{R}\) with \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) \textit{sigmoidal} that is
\begin{align*}
\psi(t)\to
    \begin{cases}
    1\quad\text{ as } t\to +\infty\\
    0\quad\text{ as } t\to -\infty\;.
    \end{cases}
\end{align*}
Notice that \(\psi\) is necessarily non-linear. 
This class of maps is of central importance to us in this section and the remainder of this article; we introduce them with the following definition:
\begin{definition}\label{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}
    Let \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be a sigmoidal map and \(k\in\mathbb{N}\). Then we define
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\psi(A(\cdot)):A:\mathbb{R}^k\to\mathbb{R}\text{ affine linear}\right\}\;.
    \end{equation}
    We call elements of \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\): shallow feedforward neural networks with activation function \(\psi\).
\end{definition}
In \cref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}, the word "shallow" refers to the fact that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) involves neural networks with one hidden layer. 
For a more general notion of neural networks, including multiple layers, see \parencite[Def.~4.1]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}.
Moreover, every affine linear map \(A:\mathbb{R}^k\to\mathbb{R}\) can be written in the form 
\begin{equation}
    A(x)=w^{tr}x+b\quad w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}\;.
\end{equation}
Thus, for \(G\in \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\), we can write
\begin{equation}
    G(x)=\sum_{j=1}^Nc_j\psi(w_j^{tr}x+b_j)\;.
\end{equation}
We call \(\left\{w_{ij}\right\}_{(i,j)\in[k]\times [N]}\) the weights of \(G\) and \(\left\{b_{j}\right\}_{j\in [N]}\) the biases. 
\\\\
This is the simplest class of neural networks one can define. As it is the only class of neural networks of interest in this article, we shall refer to them plainly as \textit{neural networks}. 
\begin{remark}\label{rem:NN don't form algebra}
    Notice that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is a vector space by definition, but it is not closed under pointwise multiplication. In particular, \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) doesn't form an algebra of functions.
\end{remark}
The first question we will choose to study is whether continuous functions on compact domains \(K\subset\mathbb{R}^k\) can be approximated by elements of \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) with respect to the \(\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in K}|f(x)|\). By this, we mean 
\begin{equation}
    \forall{f}\in C(K)\quad\forall\epsilon>0\quad\exists G\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k):\lVert f-G\rVert_{\infty}<\epsilon\;.
\end{equation}
or in topological notation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:density of NN in C(K)}
    \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_\infty}=C(K;\mathbb{R})\;.
\end{equation}
Note that this says nothing about the efficiency of the approximation for a given amount of computational resources. Concretely, it says nothing about the number of weights needed to approximate a given \(f\in C(K)\) to a given precision \(\epsilon\) in the supremum norm. 
\bigbreak
The main insight leading to the stated density result will be that \cref{eq:density of NN in C(K)} holds if \(\psi\) is able to distinguish signed Borel-measures on \(K\). More precisely, we shall require that \(\psi\) is such that the following linear map is injective:
\begin{align*}
    \Phi:\;&\mathcal{M}_r(K)\to \left(\textit{aff}(K)\right)^*\\
    &\mu\mapsto\left( A\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(A(x))\mu(\diff x)\right)\;,
\end{align*}
where \(\textit{aff}(K)\) denotes the set of affine linear maps on \(K\subset\mathbb{R}^k\), and \(\left(\textit{aff}(K)\right)^*\) its algebraic dual, and \(\mathcal{M}_r(K)\) denotes the finite, signed Radon-measures over the compact set \(K\). We call \(\psi\) \textit{discriminatory} if \(\Phi\) is injective; that is, if 
\begin{equation}
    \Phi(\mu)=0\implies\mu =0\;. 
\end{equation}
\begin{definition}\label{def:discriminatory maps over reals}
    We call a map \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) discriminatory if it is Borel-measurable, bounded, and if
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}\psi(w^{tr}x+b)\mu(\diff x)=0
    \end{equation}
    for all \(w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\mu\) finite, signed measure on \((\mathbb{R}^k,\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k))\), it implies that \(\mu\) is the 0-measure on \(\mathbb{R}^k\).
\end{definition}
The motivation for this definition will arise naturally in the proof of the following theorem.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in C(K)}
    Let \(\psi\) be a continuous discriminatory map, and \(K\subset\mathbb{R}^k\) a compact subset. Then
    \begin{equation}
         \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_\infty}=C(K;\mathbb{R})\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{theorem} 
Furthermore, the proof we present relies predominantly on the following corollary of the Hahn-Banach Theorem.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}
    Let \(Y\) be a linear subspace of a normed linear space \(X\). If \(Y\) is not dense in \(X\), then there exist functions \(F\in X^*\) and \(F\neq 0\) such that \(F(y)=0\) for all \(y\in Y\).
\end{lemma}
For a proof, see \parencite[Corollary.~4.8.7]{Friedman1982FoundationsModernAnalysis}. Finally, we present the proof of \cref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}
\begin{proof}
    The proof goes by contradiction. First, notice that we have \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\subset C(K;\mathbb{R})\) because \(\psi\) is continuous and bounded by assumption. Moreover, it is a linear subspace of the latter. Then, assume \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) is not dense in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) for the uniform topology. By \cref{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}, there must exist a nonzero continuous functional \(F\in C(K;\mathbb
    R)^*\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        F(f)=0\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\;.
    \end{equation}
     By Riesz-Marko-Kakutani \parencite[Thm.~7.10.4.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}, we know that \(C^*(K;\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathcal{M}_r(K)\), where \(\mathcal{M}_r(K)\) denotes the set of finite signed Radon measures on \(K\)\footnote{We follow the convention of \parencite{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}: We consider measures of finite variation, hence finite measures. A Radon measure is an inner regular Borel measure \parencite[Def.7.1.1.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}}. Hence, there exists \(\mu\in\mathcal{M}_r(K)\) such that 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:discriminatory property in proof}
     F(f)=\int_K f\diff\mu\quad\forall f\in C(K;\mathbb{R})\;.
 \end{equation}
