    Hence, because \(\psi(A)\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) for all affine linear maps \(A\) on \(K\), we have
    \begin{equation}
        \int_K \psi(w^{tr}x+b)\diff\mu(x)=0\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}\;.
    \end{equation}
Now recall that we assumed that \(\psi\) is discriminatory, which precisely states that \cref{eq:discriminatory property in proof} implies that \(\mu=0\). Thus, \(F=0\), whence the contradiction.
\end{proof}

In summary, the theorem, broadly speaking, works by contradiction through \cref{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}. The arguments are quite general and mainly require an explicit characterization of the topological dual. The notion of the discriminatory activation function is then chosen to be well suited to conclude density.
\\\\
For \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) as above, the topological dual is explicitly characterized by the Riesz-Markov-Kakutani Theorem by the insight that any continuous, linear functional on \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) is an integral operator. The discriminatory property of \(\psi\) turns out to be the natural notion in this context. We know from the Riesz Theorem for \(L^p\) spaces \parencite[Thm.~4.4.1.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory} that a similar characterization of bounded, linear functionals on \(L^p\) spaces through an integral mapping exists. The non-trivial part is to show that the same notion of discriminatory activation functions is well adapted to the \(L^p\)-case.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in L^p}
    Let \(\psi\) be a discriminatory function, and \(K\subset\mathbb{R}^k\) a \(\mu\) finite positive measure. Then
    \begin{equation}
         \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^p}}=L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\;,
    \end{equation}
    for \(p\in[1,\infty)\).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    The proof is analogous to the proof of \cref{thm:density if NN in C(K)}, simply using that by the Riesz Theorem every continuous, linear functional  \(F\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)^*\) is of the form
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:dual of L^p}
        F(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^k}fg\diff \mu\quad\forall f\in C(\mathbb{R}^k)\;,
    \end{equation} for some \(g\in L^q(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\) where \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\). \\\\
 First, notice that due to the finiteness of \(\mu\), we have that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is a linear subspace of  \(L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\). Then, exactly as in \cref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}, there exists \(F\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\) that annihilates \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\), and by \cref{eq:dual of L^p}, this implies
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}fg\diff \mu=0\quad\forall f\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^k)\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{proof}
The trick is now to set \(\sigma(B)\coloneqq\int_B g\diff \mu\) and notice that \(\sigma\) is again a finite measure. Assuming this is shown, we fall back to the same situation as in the proof of \cref{thm:density if NN in C(K)}:
\begin{equation}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}f\diff \sigma=0\quad\forall f\in L^p\;.
    \end{equation}
    Hence, we again conclude that, due to the assumption that \(\psi\) is discriminatory, we find
    \begin{equation}
        \sigma = 0\,.
    \end{equation}
    But this is again a contradiction, as we assumed \(F\neq 0\); thus, the claim. Finally, we argue why \(\sigma\) is a finite measure. 
    This follows from Hölder's inequality (for the same Hölder conjugate pair \(p,q\) as used in the representation \cref{eq:dual of L^p}) and the fact that \(\mu\) is finite by assumption:
    \begin{align*}
        |\sigma(B)|=|\int_{\mathbb{R}^k}1_B g\diff \mu|\leq \lVert 1_{B}\rVert_{L^p(\mu)}\lVert g \rVert_{L^q(\mu)}=(\mu(\mathbb{R}^k))^{1/p}\lVert g\rVert_{L^q(\mu)}<\infty\quad\forall B\subset \mathbb{R}^k\;.
    \end{align*}
    This concludes the proof.
\begin{remark}
 When proving the density of a set of functions \(\Psi\) in \(C(K;\mathbb{R)}\), one usually tries to show that \(\Psi\) is an algebra of functions, containing the constant functions and distinguishing points. Then one concludes by Stone-Weierstrass \parencite[Thm.~4.45]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}. Notice that this strategy won't work for \(\overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)}\). Indeed, as noted in \cref{rem:NN don't form algebra}, the neural networks do not form an algebra as they are not closed under point-wise multiplication. Surprisingly, as shown in \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators}, there is still a way to prove \cref{thm:density if NN in C(K)} via a Stone-Weierstrass argument. We give the broad idea here, and refer for details to \cref{rem:NN don't form algebra}. Observe that 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:multiplicatively closed NN}
     \sum_{i=1}^Nc_i\prod_{j=1}^M\psi(A_{ij(\cdot)})\;,
 \end{equation} for \(A_{ij}\) affine linear maps are most certainly closed under multiplication. Thus, showing the density of the set maps with representation \cref{eq:multiplicatively closed NN} for any finite \(N,M\in\mathbb{N}\), in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) can be done via Stone-Weierstrass. The remarkable part is that one can argue that this even implies that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) are dense in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) as well. The logical chain goes as follows:
    \begin{align*}
    &\rightarrow\text{approximate any continuous squashing function by shallow neural networks}\\&\rightarrow\text{appoximate the cosine function by shallow neural networks}\\&\rightarrow\text{apprixmate linear combinations of compositions of cosine functions with affine linear maps}
    \\&\rightarrow\text{notice that the latter froms a dense set in }C(K;\mathbb{R})\text{ via Stone-Weierstrass. Conclude}\;.
    \end{align*}
    We use the terminology \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators}. In particular, a \textit{squashing function} is a non-decreasing, sigmoidal map on \(K\). 
 For details, see the proof of \parencite[Thm.~2.4.]{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators}.
    \end{remark}
    We finish this section with a lemma showing that there are, in fact, very large classes of functions that are discriminatory
    \begin{proposition}\label{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory}
        Let \(\psi\) be bounded, measurable, and sigmoidal. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory.
    \end{proposition}
    In particular, continuous sigmoidal maps are discriminatory as well as non-decreasing sigmoidal maps\footnote{Note that non-decreasing maps have at most countably many discontinuities and are thus measurable as functions from \(\mathbb{R}\) to \([0,1]\)}. The latter is what \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators} refers to as \textit{squashing functions}.
 For the proof, we refer to \parencite[Lemma.~1]{Cybenko1989}.
 A more involved result from \parencite{Hornik1991} shows that for the notion of neural networks introduced in \cref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}, the conditions can be relaxed further
 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:non-constant implies discriminatory}
     Let \(\psi\) be bounded, measurable, and non-constant. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory.
 \end{proposition}
 For the proof, see \parencite[Thm.~5]{Hornik1991}.
\subsection{Neural Networks on Topological Vector Spaces}
We will now turn to the task of defining neural networks on more general spaces than \(\mathbb{R}^k\) and show that we can still obtain analogous density results. First, this is of independent mathematical interest. Secondly, this might be of practical interest, as one might want to consider neural networks on path-spaces, which are generally not finite-dimensional. In particular, we will use this viewpoint in our \textit{naïve} approach explained in \cref{sec:The naïv approach and what goes wrong} and in \cref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental}. 
\bigbreak
Note that if we want to generalize neural networks to be defined on more abstract spaces, such as \(\mathbb{R}^k\), for instance, locally convex spaces, it is immediately clear that we will need to generalize the notion of shallow feedforward neural networks from \cref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}, as they rely on the parametrization of affine maps on \(\mathbb{R}^k\). As previously noted, recall that for any such affine map \(A\), there exist \(w\in\mathbb{R}^k\) and \(b \in \mathbb{R}\) such that 
\begin{equation}
    A(x)=w^{tr}x+b\;.
\end{equation}
We can identify any \(w^{tr}\) with the continuous linear form \(\langle w,\cdot\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^*\) and vise versa by Riesz-Fischer \parencite[Cor.~4.3.2.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}. This motivates the following generalization of \cref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks} to topological vector spaces \(X\) with topological dual \(X^*\).
\begin{definition}\label{def:NN on locally convex X}
    Let \(X\) be a topological vector space and \(\mathcal{A}\) a \(\sigma\)-algebra on \(X\). Furthermore, let \(\Gamma\) be a set of real-valued, \(\mathcal{A}\)-measurable maps on \(X\) and \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be measurable and sigmoidal. Then we define 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(X,\Gamma)\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\psi(F(\cdot)+b):F\in \Gamma,b\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\;.
    \end{equation}
    We call elements of \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(X,\Gamma)\) shallow feedforward neural networks with the activation function \(\psi\).
\end{definition}
In particular, elements in \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(X,\Gamma)\) are \(\mathcal{A}\)-measurable functions.
\\\\
\textbf{Example:} We shall often be interested in the case where \(\Gamma=X^*\). If we take \(X=C(K;\mathbb{R})\) for \(K\subset\mathbb{R}^k\) compact, by Riesz-Markov-Kakutani, elements in \cref{def:NN on locally convex X} take a much more explicit form:
\begin{equation}
     \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(C(K;\mathbb{R}),C(K;\mathbb{R})^*)=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{f\mapsto\psi(\int_X f\diff \mu+b):\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K),b\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\;.
\end{equation}
We obtain a similar characterization for \(X=L^p(\mu)\) by Riesz. 
\begin{equation}
     \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(L^p(\mu),(L^p(\mu))^*)=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{f\mapsto\psi(\int_X fg\diff \mu+b):g\in L^q(\mu)(K),b\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\;,
\end{equation}
where \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\).

We shall now give the analog density result to \cref{thm:density of NN in L^p} for locally convex spaces \(X\) instead of the common Euclidean spaces \(\mathbb{R}^k\). For what is to follow, we shall assume:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(X\) is a topological vector space
    \item\(\Gamma = X^*\), where \(X^*\) denotes the topological dual of \(X\), that is \(X^*=\left\{\text{continuous, linear maps: }X\to\mathbb{R}\right\}\)
    \item\(\mathcal{A}=\sigma(X^*)\)\;.
\end{itemize}
Under this assumption, we will omit \(\Gamma =X^*\) in the notation of \cref{def:NN on locally convex X}.
Note that the proof given for \cref{thm:density of NN in L^p} doesn't require any property of the \(\mathbb{R}^k\) only the discriminatory property of \(\psi\). The latter admits the obvious generalization
\begin{definition}\label{def:discriminatory property of X}
 We call a map \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) discriminatory if it is Borel-measurable, bounded, and if
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{X}\psi(F(x)+b)\mu(\diff x)=0
    \end{equation}
    for all \(F\in X^*,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\mu\) a finite, signed measure on \((X,\sigma(X^*))\), it follows that \(\mu=0\).    
\end{definition}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space}
    Let \(\psi\) be a discriminatory map, \(\mu\) a finite, positive measure, and \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Then
    \begin{equation}
         \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(X)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^p}}=L^p(X,\mathcal{A},\mu)\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
    Notice that \(\psi\), being discriminatory, it is in particular bounded. Hence \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K
    X)\subset L^p(X,\mu)\) because \(\mu\) is finite by assumption. The rest of the proof is completely analogous to \cref{thm:density of NN in L^p} and relies on the fact that for a discriminatory \(\psi\) in the sense of \cref{def:discriminatory property of X}, Hahn-Banach yields that the inner regular Borel measure \(\mu\) is the \(0\) measure. Hence, we indeed need the discriminatory property 
\end{proof}
The non-trivial part that requires more work is the generalization of \cref{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory}. 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}
    Let \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be measurable, bounded, and sigmoidal. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory in the sense of \cref{def:discriminatory property of X}.
\end{proposition}
Before giving the proof, we state a useful Lemma from \parencite[Lemma.~7.13.5.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}. For the statement, we require the definition of the characteristic functional of a measure \(\mu\) on \(Cyl(X,\Gamma)\) for \(\Gamma\) a subset of real-valued maps on \(X\) and \(Cyl(X,\Gamma)\) defined as follows:
\begin{equation}
    Cyl(X,\Gamma)=\left\{ C(f_1,\dots,f_n,B):f_i\in \Gamma\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n,n\in\mathbb{N},B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^n)\right\}\;,
\end{equation}
where \(C(f_1,\dots,f_n,B)=\left\{x\in X:(f_1(x),\dots,f_2(x))\in B)\right\}\) is called a \(\Gamma\)-cylinder set. Clearly, we have
\begin{equation}
    \sigma(Cyl(X,\Gamma))=\sigma(\Gamma)\;. 
\end{equation}
We define the Fourier transform of a measure \(\mu\) on \(Cyl(X,\Gamma)\) as 
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{\mu}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it}\mu\circ f^{-1}(dt)\;.\footnotemark
\end{equation}\footnotetext{Notice that this definition is consistent with the definition of the Fourier transform of measures on \(\mathbb{R}^n\): \(\tilde{\mu}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\langle y,x\rangle}\mu(\diff x)\). The equivalence follows from the identification through Riesz-Fischer for the Hilbert-space \((\mathbb{R}^n,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)\)}
From here on, we shall take \(\Gamma=X^*\).
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Fourier equality iif measure equality for general X}
    Let \(X\) be a topological vector space and \(X^*\) its topological dual. Let \(\mu,\nu\) be measures on \(\sigma(X^*)\) and \(\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{\nu}\), then one has \(\mu=\nu\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For all \(f_1,\dots,f_n\in X^*\) we have 
    \begin{align*}
        \widetilde{\mu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}}&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}e^{i\langle y,x\rangle} \mu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}(\diff t)\\&=\int_X e^{i\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x)}\mu(\diff x)=\widetilde{\mu}(\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x))=\widetilde{\nu}(\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x))\\&=\widetilde{\nu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}}\;.
    \end{align*}
 Now, from \parencite[Prop.~3.8.6]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}, this implies 
    \begin{equation}
        \mu(\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x))=\nu(\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x))\;.
    \end{equation}
    But by definition, \(\sigma(X^*)\) is generated by the intersection stable generating system of cylinder sets \(C(f_1,\dots,f_n,B)\), hence \(\mu=\nu\) on all of \(\sigma(X^*)\). Now this shows that 
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:radon measures uniquely determined by values on sigma(X*)}
For locally convex Hausdorff spaces \(X\) \cref{lemma:Fourier equality iif measure equality for general X} also holds true for Radon measures; that is, inner regular Borel-measures. This is not trivial, as in general for topological vector spaces \(\mathcal{B}(X)\subset \sigma(X^*)\). It follows from \parencite[sec.~7.1]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory} and, in particular, the discussion at the beginning of subsection 7.12 in Bogachev that Radon measures on locally convex spaces are uniquely determined by their values of \(Cyl(X,X^*)=\sigma(X^*)\).
\end{remark}
We can finally turn to the proof of \cref{prop:necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}.
\begin{proof}
    The proof goes by contradiction. Assume there is such a non-zero, finite measure \(\nu=\nu^+-\nu^-\). We show that the conditions in the proposition imply that \(\nu^+=\nu^-\), hence \(\nu=0\), which contradicts the assumption\(\psi\) is discriminatory. We do this by showing the equivalent claim 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:proof strategy of necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}
        \widetilde{\nu}^+(f)=\widetilde{\nu}^-(f)\quad\forall f\in X^*\;.
    \end{equation} Indeed, equivalence follows from the previously stated \cref{lemma:Fourier equality iif measure equality for general X}. We show \cref{eq:proof strategy of necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property} by a monotone class argument.
    \bigbreak
 More precisely, notice that
    \begin{equation}
        \widetilde{\nu}^+(f)-\widetilde{\nu}^-(f)=\widetilde{\nu}(f)=\int_X e^{if(x)}\mu(\diff x)=\int_X \cos(f(x))\nu(\diff x)+i\int_X\sin(f(x))\nu(\diff x)\;,
    \end{equation}
    hence to show that this expression is equal to \(0\), we need to show that the complex number on the right is equal to \(0\), that is, that both its real and imaginary parts are vanishing. We set up the monotone class argument:
    Let \(\mathcal{H}\) be the class of
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal{H}=\left\{ \text{bounded, measurable } h: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} \text{ such that } \int_X h(f(x)) \nu(\diff x) = 0 \; \forall f \in X^* \right\}\;.
    \end{align*}
    The goal is to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all maps \(1_I, I=(a,b]\) for \(a\leq b\)\footnote{We take the convention \((a,b]=\emptyset\) for \(a=b\).}. It is well known that these form a generating, intersection stable system for \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\). If we show the latter, then by the monotone class theorem \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded measurable maps; hence, both integrals for \(h\in\left\{\cos,\sin\right\}\) vanish, proving the claim.
    \\\\
    We will need to argue why \(\mathcal{H}\) is a monotone class and why it contains the respective indicator functions. Recall that we assumed, by contradiction, that \(\psi\) is not discriminatory. Hence, there exists \(\nu\neq 0\) such that \(\int_X\psi(f(x)+b)\nu(\diff x)=0\quad\forall f\in X^*,b\in\mathbb{R}\).\bigbreak
    \(\mathcal{H}\) is a real-vector space, containing constant functions and closed under  bounded monotone limits. Indeed, the first claim is clear by definition. It contains \(1_{\mathbb{R}}\) since for every \(f\in X^*\) we have 
    \begin{equation}
        \int_X1_{\mathbb{R}}(f(x))\nu(\diff x)=\nu(X)=\frac{1}{\psi(b)}\int_X\psi(0+b)\nu(\diff x)=0\;,
    \end{equation}
    for any \(b\in\mathbb{R}\) such that \(\psi(b)\neq 0\), which exists since \(\psi\) is sigmoidal and with \(0\in X^*\).
    For the bounded monotone limits, we conclude by dominated convergence for the positive and negative parts of \(\nu\). Indeed, let \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset \mathcal{H}\) be uniformly bounded by \(C\in\mathbb{R}_+\), converging pointwise to \(f\). Then \(f\) is bounded and measurable as well, and 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\int_Xh_n(f(x))\nu^+(\diff x)-\int_Xh_n(f(x))\nu^-(\diff x)\right)=0+0\quad\forall f\in X^*\;,
    \end{equation}
    by dominated convergence. It remains to show that \(1_{(a,b]}\) for \(a\leq b\) are in \(\mathcal{H}\). It is enough to show that \(1_{(c,\infty)}\in\mathcal{H}\) for any \(c\in\mathbb{R}\). Then, because \(\mathcal{H}\) is a vector space of real-valued functions, the claim follows. Indeed, consider a real sequence \((s_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) increasing to \(\infty\). Then:
    \begin{align}\label{eq:concluding monotone class for discriminatory functions}
        &\int_X1_{(c,\infty)}(f(x))\nu(\diff x)=\nu(f(x)>c)=\lim_{\gamma\to-\infty}\nu(f(x)>c)+\psi(\gamma)\nu(f(x)=c)\\&=\lim_{\gamma\to-\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_X\psi(s_n(f(x)-c)+\gamma)\nu(\diff x)\;.
    \end{align}
    Indeed, the second equality holds by the sigmoidality of \(\psi\), and the second equality uses the fact that \(\psi\) is bounded by assumption; thus, by dominated convergence 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_X\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta))\nu(\diff x)=\int_X\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta))\nu(\diff x)\;,
        \end{equation}
        with \(\psi_n=\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta))\leq\lVert \psi\rVert_{\infty}<\infty\),  for the positive and negative parts of \(\nu\). Indeed, if \(f(x)>c\), then \(s_n(f(x)-c)\to\infty\) as \(n\to\infty\), and by the sigmoidality of \(\psi\) we have that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-c)+\gamma)=1\). If \(f(x)<c\), by the same argument: \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-c)=0\), and for \(f(x)=c\), \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-c)=\psi(\gamma)\). But the left hand side in \cref{eq:concluding monotone class for discriminatory functions} is also equal to \(0\) by assumption; whence the claim.
\end{proof}



By \cref{rem:radon measures uniquely determined by values on sigma(X*)}, we have the following corollary for a more restrictive notion of a discriminatory map
\begin{definition}\label{def:Borel discriminatory property of X}
 We call a map \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) Borel-discriminatory if it is Borel-measurable, bounded, and if
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{X}\psi(F(x)+b)\mu(\diff x)=0
    \end{equation}
    for all \(F\in X^*,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\mu\), a finite signed Radon measure on \((X,\mathcal{B}(X))\) implies that \(\mu=0\).    
\end{definition}
\begin{corollary}
     Let \(X\) be a locally convex Hausdorff space and \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be measurable, bounded, and sigmoidal. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory in the sense of \cref{def:Borel discriminatory property of X}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{theorem}
    Let \(X\) be a locally convex Hausdorff space, \(\psi\) a continuous, Borel-discriminatory map, and \(K\subset X\) a compact subspace. Then
    \begin{equation}
       \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}}=C(K;\mathbb{R})
    \end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Notice that since every \(G\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) is of the form 
    \begin{equation}
        G(x) =\sum_{i=1}^Nc_i\psi(F(x)+b)\;,
    \end{equation}
    where \(\psi\) is continuous by assumption, and \(F\in X^*\) is hence in particular continuous. Hence \(G:X\to\mathbb{R}\) is continuous as a composition of continuous maps.
 The notion of a Borel-discriminatory map is such that the proof is now analogous to \cref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}. Indeed, the argument by contradiction essentially works by using the discriminatory property of \(\psi\) to argue that the Radon measure \(\mu\) associated with the functionals \(F\in C(K;\mathbb{R})^*\) must vanish, yielding a contradiction with the fact that \(F\) is a non-zero functional. 
 \end{proof}
 \begin{remark}
     Observe that the previous notion of a discriminatory map \cref{def:discriminatory property of X} is not sufficient for this purpose, as the latter only asserts that the measure \(\mu\) vanishes on \(\sigma(X^*)\). For a Borel measure \(\mu\) on \(\mathcal{B}(X)\), where \(X\) is a general topological vector space, this is not sufficient to conclude that \(\mu=0\). Nevertheless, in the proof of \cref{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space} this is not needed, as for a finite, positive measure \(\mu\) on \(\sigma(X^*)\), the measure 
     \begin{equation}
     \sigma(B)=\int_B g\diff \mu,\;B\in\sigma(X^*),    
     \end{equation} is itself a finite signed measure on \(\sigma(X^*)\) by the same argument as in \cref{thm:density of NN in L^p}. 
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}\label{def:standing assumptions}
    We refer to the following set of requirements as \textit{standing assumptions}:
    \begin{itemize}
    \item \(X\) is a topological vector space with topology \(\tau_X\).
    \item \(\Gamma=X^*\)\;.
    \item \(\mathcal{A}=\sigma(X^*)\), where \(X^*\) is the topological dual of \(X\).
    \item \(\mu\) is a finite, signed-measure on \(\mathcal{A}\).
    \item \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) is bounded, measurable, and sigmoidal. 
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}


\chapter{Continuous-time Mean-Variance Hedging}\label{ch:continuous time mean variance hedging}
In this section, we explore we discuss mean-variance hedging in a mathematical finance context and study some properties in special cases, which we summarize by the properties of the price process used: a square integrable martingale setting and a continuous semimartingale setting. In particular, we assume a continuous time setting. Hence, we fix the stochastic basis for this chapter as a complete, filtered probability space
\begin{equation}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P}) 
\end{equation}
with filtration \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\), where \(\mathbb{T}_+\subset\mathbb{R}_+\). For this chapter, except \cref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}, we allow \(T=+\infty\). We assume that \(\mathcal{F}\) satisfies the usual conditions. In particular, when we speak of martingales, we shall take its càdlàg version.
\section{Self financing strategies in continuous times}\label{sec:Self financing strategies in continuous times}
In the continuous time setting, i.e. \(\mathbb{T}_+\subset\mathbb{R_+}\), the gains process of a self-financing strategy \cref{eq:self financing strategy} is given by
\begin{equation}
    V_t = V_0 + \int_0^t \vartheta_s\diff X_s\;,t\in\mathbb{T}_+
\end{equation}
where \(\int_0^t \vartheta_s\diff X_s\) denotes the stochastic integral of strategy \(\vartheta\) with respect to the price process \(X\). Thus, we need to understand stochastic integration with respect to stochastic processes in order to understand self-financing strategies in continuous time mathematical finance and, in particular, mean-variance hedging. The definition of the stochastic integral with respect to \(X\) depends on the degree of generality of \(X\), which in turn determines the set of integrable processes \(L(X)\) with respect to which the stochastic integral is well defined. Different instances of \(L(X)\) will be encountered in the proceeding discussion of \cref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square integrable Martingales} and \cref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square integrable Martingales}. 
\\\\
In the case where \(X\) is of finite variation, the integration theory is classical, pathwise Lebesgue-Stieltjes integration with respect to the induced signed measure on \(\mathbb{R}\):
\begin{equation}
    \diff X(\omega)=\diff X_{+}(\omega)-\diff X_{-}(\omega)
\end{equation}
where \(X=X_+-X_-\) is the decomposition of \(X\) into two non-decreasing processes \(X_+,X_-\). For non-decreasing processes, we can associate a positive measure, namely Lebesgue-Stieltjes measures.
If \(X\) is of infinite variation, this no longer works. For Hölder continuous integration, we might still be able to define a sensible integral in a similar way (Young integral: see \parencite{Young1936}). We process with more pathological behavior, such as Brownian motion; we can't classically define an integration theory. In particular, we can't define a pathwise integral with respect to Brownian motion (see \parencite[Thm.~56]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). We will discuss some aspects of the construction of the stochastic integral in some generality, although not all generality. 
\bigbreak 
We shall distinguish between the statistical, or real-world, probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\)
and equivalent probability measures under which the discounted price process becomes a
local martingale. We call a probability measure \(\mathbb{Q}\sim \mathbb{P}\) an equivalent local martingale
measure (ELMM) for \(X\) if \(X\) is a local \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale and denote the set of all such measures by
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal M_e(X)
    :=
    \{\mathbb{Q}\sim \mathbb{P} : X \text{ is a local } \mathbb{Q}\text{-martingale}\}.
\end{equation}
Elements of \(\mathcal M_e(X)\) are also called risk-neutral measures. We shall assume that \(\mathcal{M}_e(X)\neq\emptyset\), which according to \parencite[Cor~.1.2.]{DelbaenSchachermayer1994FTAP} is equivalent to assuming that there is \textit{no free lunch with vanishing risk} (NFLVR). In a complete market,
the equivalent local martingale measure is unique, while in incomplete markets, one
typically has several such measures. 
\bigbreak
From the perspective of integration theory, we might allow all \(X\)-integrable strategies \(\vartheta\). For our treatment of mean-variance hedging, we already argued that it is a good idea to restrict to a class of admissible integrands \(\Theta\) such that the respective stochastic integral, evaluated at the final time, is itself a square integrable random variable. For the purpose of this article, this shall be the relevant restriction on \(\vartheta\). We mention at this point that, from a mathematical finance perspective, one might want to restrict the set of admissible strategies further. A common financial admissibility condition, such as that found in \parencite[Def.~3.4.]{RheinlaenderSexton2011HedgingDerivatives}, is to require that admissible strategies are such that the associated value process, and thus, up to its initial value, the stochastic integral with respect to the price process, shall be a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-supermartingale for every \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_e\). In particular, a strategy \(\vartheta\) that is locally bounded, predictable, and bounded from below is admissible. Indeed, for locally bounded and predictable strategies, the value process is a local martingale (see \parencite[Thm.~29]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}), and by Fatou, boundedness from below implies that it is a super-martingale. 
\bigbreak 
This notion of admissible strategies prohibits pathological examples such as the suicide strategy \parencite[Ex.~3.3.]{RheinlaenderSexton2011HedgingDerivatives} yielding a sure profit with unlimited risk. In this article, we are concerned with mean-variance hedging as a mathematical problem more than its financial interpretation; thus, we value generality over practicality. In short, we shall define \textit{admissible} strategies as a set \(\Theta\) of strategies, restricted in order to make the mathematical problem at hand well-defined and/or to develop a solution theory. Hence, the standing convention is:
\begin{equation}
    \vartheta \text{ admissible }\iff\vartheta\in\Theta\subset L(X)\;.
\end{equation}
This is to be taken as the baseline notion of admissibility and will be restricted depending on the current need. 

\section{Square integrable martingale case}
In this section, we will solve the mean-variance problem \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} in the square integrable martingale setting, which assumes the price process \(M\) to be a locally square integrable local martingale. That is there exists a sequence of stopping times \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) increasing to \(+\infty\) almost surely, such that 
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}|M^{\tau_n}1_{\tau_n>0}|]<\infty\;.\footnotemark
\end{equation}\footnotetext{We take on the convention used in \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} with respect to locality. The indicator mainly solves integrability issues at the initial time. }
\bigbreak
One might oppose this approach from a financial context, as it suggests a symmetric treatment of losses and gains, which does not usually correspond to how a real agent acts in the market. However, note that assuming the price process to be a local martingale amounts to considering the mean-variance hedging problem \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} under the risk-neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\) instead of the statistical measure \(P\). Hence, seen under the statistical measure, the problem need not be symmetric.\footnote{We assume an incomplete market without arbitrage; hence, there exist multiple equivalent martingale measures} . Furthermore, even from a purely theoretical point of view, the square integrable martingale case constitutes arguably the cornerstone of general stochastic integration, that is, stochastic integration with respect to the largest class of stochastic processes, for which one can define a sensible notion of an integral. It turns out that these are exactly the set of classical semimartingales \parencite[Thm.~47,Ch.III]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}. Finally, treating the square-integrable martingale case is insightful, as we shall see that in this case, the explicit optimal mean-variance solution is closely linked to the Kunita-Watanabe decomposition of the liability.
\\\\
In this section, we sketch the construction of the stochastic integral for square integrable price processes and appropriate integrands. For this, we define the space \(\mathcal{H}^p\) and call elements in \(\mathcal{H}^p\); \(p\)-integrable martingales.

\subsection{Stochastic Integration for square integrable Martingales}\label{sec:Stochastic Integration for square integrable Martingales}
The strategy will be as follows: we will start by defining integration with respect to \(X\) for \textit{simple processes}. We will find that the so constructed integration map defines an isomoprhism of Hilbert-spaces which can be used to extend the ingral by continuity to more general integrands.
\\\\
 First, we shall motivate why the square integrable martingale case can be considered the heart of general stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales. For this we first explain what we mean by semimartingales in the first place.
We call a stochastic process \(X\) a semimartingale, or a classical semimartingale if it can be written as 
\begin{equation}\label{def:semimartingale}
    X = X_0+M+A\;
\end{equation}
with \(M\in\mathcal{M}_{0,loc}\) and \(A\) of finite variation. We call \(X\) special if we can choose \(A\) in \cref{def:semimartingale} to be predictable. In that case, the decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\) is unique and is referred to as the canonical decomposition of \(X\).
\\\\
For a classical semimartingale, according to the fundamental theorem of local martingales \parencite[Thm.~29,Ch.III]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, every local martingale can be decomposed as a sum of a local martingale with bounded jumps and a local martingale of finite variation. The latter can be absorbed in the finite variation part of \cref{def:semimartingale}. Hence, we may choose the local martingale \(M\) to have bounded jumps and thus itself a be locally square integrable local martingale. Assume that integration with respect to locally square integrable processes \(M\) is understood for a respective class of integrable processes. Up to the non-uniqueness of the decomposition \cref{def:semimartingale} of a classical semimartingale, we naturally define the integral of some \(X\)-integrable process \(\vartheta\) with respect to \(X\) as 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heuristic integral with respect to semimartingale}
    \int \vartheta\diff X\coloneqq\int \vartheta\diff M+\int H\diff A\;.
\end{equation}
In particular, integration with respect to finite variation measures is well understood via the Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral. Hence, the main conceptual gap in \cref{eq:heuristic integral with respect to semimartingale} is, up to localization, the construction of \(\int \vartheta\diff M\) for a square integrable martingale \(M\).
\subsection{\(\mathcal{H}^p\) martingale spaces}
In order to discuss stochastic integration with respect to square integrable martingales, we need to introduce and study the martingale space \(\mathcal{H}^2\) and its geometry. We will start with a general discussion about \(\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\). 
\\\\
In this subsection, we allow and choose \(T=\infty\). This is justified because martingales in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(p\geq 1\) turn out to be uniformly integrable martingales; hence, by \parencite[Thm.~12.27]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}, the almost sure limit at \(T=\infty\) exists. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:H^p}
    Let \(p\in[1,\infty)\), and write
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}\coloneqq \lVert M_{T}^*\rVert_{p}=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}|M_t|^p]^{1/p}\;,
    \end{equation}
    where we use the notation \(M^*_{T}\coloneqq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}|M_t|\) and \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_p\) to denote the \(L^p\)-norm. \(\mathcal{H}^p\) then denotes the space of martingales such that 
    \begin{equation}
         \lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}<\infty\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}
We shall denote by \(\mathcal{H}^p_0\), processes \(X\) in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) with \(X_0=0\). The following lemma will be crucial in characterizing the space \(\mathcal{H}^p\).
\begin{remark}\label{rem:equivalence of H^p convetions}
    There exist different conventions for defining \(\mathcal{H}^p\) in the literature. Another common convetion is to define \(\mathcal{H}^p\) as the space of martingales such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\mathbb{E}[|M_t|^p]<\infty\;.
    \end{equation}
    Nevertheless, notice that due to Jensen's inequality, \(|X|^p\) is a submartingale and due to Doob's \(L^p\)-inequality for submartingales for \(p>1\) we have
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}|M_t|^p]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq q\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\mathbb{E}[|M_t|^p]^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty\;,
    \end{equation}
    where \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\). The converse inequality is always true, hence for \(p>1\) both convetions on the space of martingales, are equivalent. The upside of this way of defining the \(\mathcal{H}^p\) space is by requiring that a local martingales in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) is automatically a true martingale by \cref{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale}.
\end{remark}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:order of H^p spaces}
 Let \(\mathcal{H}^p\) be as in \cref{def:H^p}. Then \(\mathcal{H}^p\) has the following property
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For \(q\leq p\), then \(\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^q}\leq\lVert X\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}\) thus \(\mathcal{H}^p\subset\mathcal{H}^q\). In particular, \(\mathcal{H}^p\subset\mathcal{H}^1\) for all \(p\in[1,\infty)\)\;.
        \item If \(p\in(1,\infty)\) and \(M\) are uniformly integrable, then 
        \begin{equation}
            \lVert M_{T}\rVert_p\leq\lVert M_{T}^*\rVert_p\leq q\lVert M_{T}\rVert_p
        \end{equation}
        for \(p,q\) Hölder-conjugates, that is \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\)\;.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    (1) is the usual inclusion of \(L^p(\mu)\) spaces for finite measure \(\mu\) and follows by Jensen's inequality \parencite[Thm.~4.3]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}. For (2), first notice that by the Martingale convergence Theorem \(M_{T}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to \infty}M_n\) is well defined as an almost sure limit. The first inequality is obvious, and the second follows by Doob's \(L^p\) inequality \parencite[Prop.~2.17.]{RheinlaenderSexton2011HedgingDerivatives}. 
\end{proof}
For the following lemma, we need an easy criterion for which a local martingale is actually a true martingale. Furthermore, it gives sufficient conditions for a local martingale to be a uniformly integrable martingale
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale}
    Let \(X\) be a local martingale such that \(\mathbb{E}[X_t^*]<\infty\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{T}_+\). Then \(X\) is a martingale. If \(E[X_T^*]<\infty\), then \(X\) is a uniformly integrable martingale. 
\end{lemma}
For a proof, see \parencite[Thm.~5.1, II]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:martingales in H^p are UI}
    For all \(p\in[1,\infty)\), we have that \(X\in\mathcal{H}^p\) is uniformly integrable and \(|M_t|^p\to|M_{T}|^p\) in \(L^1\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For the first part, it suffices to notice that \(X\in\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) implies  \(E[X_T^*]<\infty\), by point (1) of \cref{lemma:order of H^p spaces}. Thus, \(M\) is uniformly integrable, and \(M_{T}\) exists as an almost sure limit \(\lim_{t\to T}M_t=M_{T}\). Now, because \(|M_t|^p\leq (M_{T}^*)^p\) and \((M_{T}^*)^p\) are integrable, the family \((|M_t|^p)_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\) is uniformly integrable by \parencite[Example.~2.5.3.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} for \(\phi(\cdot)=|\cdot|^p\). Hence, the almost sure convergence of \(|M_t|^p\) to \(|M_{T}|^p\) implies the respective \(L^1\) convergence of the latter.
\end{proof}
Now, without proof, we state the following theorem. A proof is given in \parencite[Lemma~10.1.5.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:H^p is a Banach space}
    Identifying indistinguishable martingales, \(\mathcal{H}^p\) is a Banach space under its norm for all \(p\in[1,\infty)\)\;.
\end{theorem}
We can use our general insight from \cref{lemma:order of H^p spaces} and \cref{thm:H^p is a Banach space} to identify \(\mathcal{H}^p\) with the Banach space \(L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\). Indeed, the identification is explicitly provided by the following bijective mapping
\begin{align}\label{eq:H^p identification}
    &\mathcal{H}^p\to L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\mathbb{P})\\
    &M\mapsto M_{T}
\end{align}
First of all, because the processes in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) are uniformly integrable by the previous result \cref{lemma:martingales in H^p are UI}, the map is well defined. Injectivity again follows from the fact that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) is uniformly integrable, and hence by \parencite[Thm.12.27]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses} the limit \(M_{T}=\lim_{n\to\infty} M_t\) exists and \(M_t = \mathbb{E}[M_{T}|\mathcal{F}_t]\). Surjectivity follows by setting \(M_t = \mathbb{E}[X|\mathcal{F}_t]\in\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(X\in L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\mathbb{P})\). Moreover, \cref{lemma:order of H^p spaces} (2) states that the norm on \(\mathcal{H}^p\) is equivalent to the norm on \(L^p\) under the stated identification. In particular, this implies that the dual of \(\mathcal{H}^p\) can be \(\mathcal{H}^q\) where \((p,q)\) are taken to be Hölder conjugates. More explicitly, any continuous linear form \(\phi\in(\mathcal{H}^p)^*\) can be identified with some \(N\in\mathcal{H}^q\) and written as \(\phi(M)=\mathbb{E}[M_{T}N_{T}]\). 
\\\\
The identification of \(\mathcal{H}^p\) with \(L^p\) suggests that the case \(p=2\) has a particularly rich Hilbert space structure. According to the identification \cref{eq:H^p identification}, \(\mathcal{H}^2\) can be identified with the Hilbert space \(L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\mathbb{P})\). Explicitly, we can define an inner product on \(\mathcal{H}^2\) that is given by 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar product on H^2}
    (M,N)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=\mathbb{E}[M_{T}N_{T}]\;.
\end{equation}
This inner product induces the same topology as the \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}\)-norm in \cref{def:H^p} and yields a natural notion of orthogonality. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:weak orthogonality}
    We call \(X,Y\in\mathcal{H}^2\) \textit{weakly orthogonal} if \((X,Y)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=0\)\;.
\end{definition}
The terminology \textit{weak} suggests that there might be a related \textit{strong} orthogonality. Indeed, this is the case and shall be introduced in \cref{sec:Kunita-Watanabe Decomposition}.
\newpage
\subsubsection{Integration of simple integrands}
This general approach requires minimal conditions on the integrator \(X\), which we require to be a classical semimartingale. It follows the general insight of integration theory to define an integral for piece-wise constant functions and later argue in what way to extend it. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:elementary predictable process}
    We call \(H\) an \textit{elementary predictable process} if there exists a finite, increasing sequence of deterministic times, \(0=t_0<\dots<t_{n+1}<\infty\) and a sequence of bounded random variables \((H^i)_{i=0}^n\subset\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{t_i},\mathbb{P})\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        H = H^01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^nH^i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}\;.
    \end{equation}
In particular, we have that \(H^i\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{t_i}\)-measurable. We denote the set of elementary predictable processes by \(\mathcal{E}\) and we say \(H\in\mathcal{E}\) is associated with a sequence \((t_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\).
\end{definition}
Notice that elementary processes are, as defined, adapted, left-continuous processes; thus, they are predictable. In particular, observe that clearly \(\mathcal{E}\) is closed under summation, but even more, it is also closed under pointwise multiplication. To see this, take two elements of \(H,G\in\mathcal{E}\) and their associated sequences \((t^H_k)_{n=0}^{n+1}\), \((t^G_k)_{k=0}^{l+1}\) for some \(n,l\in\mathbb{N}\). They both form a partition of \([0,\infty]\) if we add one point \(t_{n+2}=t_{l+2}=\infty\) to both sequences. Take their common refinement and notice that the pointwise product of two indicator functions of disjoint sets vanishes.
\\\\
For later use, we denote by \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\) the set of processes as in \cref{def:elementary predictable process}, but for infinite sets \((t_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) where \(0=t_0<\dots<t_n<\infty\) and \(\lim_{n\to\infty}t_n=\infty\). Hence, processes of the form 
\begin{equation}
      H = H^01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i= 0}^\infty H^i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}\;.
\end{equation}
We identify a process in \(\mathcal{E}\) associated with the sequence \((t_k)_{k=0}^n\), with the process in \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\) and the corresponding sequence \(\tilde{t}_t=t_k\quad k=0,\dots,n\), and \(t_k = t_n\) for \(k>n\), following the convention \(1_{(t,t]}=1_{\emptyset}=0\), effectively identifying \(\mathcal{E}\) as a subset of \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\).
\bigbreak
The corresponding integral of \(H\) with respect to \(X\) is then naturally defined as follows
\begin{definition}\label{def:integral of simple processes}
Let \(H\in\mathcal{S}\). Then we set 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:integral of simple processes}
    I_X(H)\coloneqq H^0X_0+\sum_{i=1}^n H^i(X_{\tau_{i+1}}-X_{\tau_{i}})\;.
\end{equation}
Furthermore, for every \(t\in\mathbb{T}_+\), we define
    \begin{equation}
        I_X(H)_t\coloneqq H^0X_0+\sum_{i=1}^n H^i(X_{\tau_{i+1}\wedge t}-X_{\tau_{i}\wedge t})\;.
    \end{equation}
    We call \(I_X(H)\) the stochastic integral process of \(H\) with respect to \(X\) and use the following notations interchangeably
    \begin{equation}
        I_X(H)=H\bullet X=\int H\diff X\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}
Notice that the expressions \cref{eq:integral of simple processes} are pathwise and hence independent of the representation of \(H\in\mathcal{S}\).
\subsubsection{Stochastic Integral for locally square integrable local martingales}
 a square integrable martingale \(M\). The case where \(M\) is a locally square integrable local martingale follows from a localization argument. 
\\\\
The key observation is the so called \textit{Itô Isometry}, which we state below. 
Recall that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2\) one can define its quadratic variation process \(\langle M\rangle\) \parencite[Def.11.1.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}\footnote{For our purposes, think of \(\langle M\rangle\) as the unique compensator \parencite[Thm.…18,Ch.III]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} for the quadratic variation process \([M]\). That is, \(\langle M\rangle\) is the unique predictable, finite variation process such that \([M]-\langle M\rangle\) is a local martingale.}. The crucial observation is the following.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes}
    For \(H\in\mathcal{E}\) and \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\), we have that \(\int H\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and the property
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes}
        \mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^{T} H_s\diff M_s\right)^2]=\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{T}H_s^2\diff\langle M\rangle_s]\;,
    \end{equation}
    where \(\int_0^{T}H_s^2\diff\langle M\rangle_s\) denotes the pathwise Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral for the finite variation process \(\langle M\rangle\).
\end{proposition}
The statement involves two sub-statements. First, it postulates the equality \cref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes}, secondly it states that \(\int H\diff M\) is a square integrable martingale starting at \(0\), hence the terms in \cref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes} are finite. 
The proof of the martingale property, as well as the equality in \cref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes}, is proven by direct calculation \parencite[Lemma.~12.1.4]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}.
The main idea is that one wants to elevate \cref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} to an isometry statement. From our identification \cref{eq:scalar product on H^2}, we have
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^{T} H_s\diff M_s\right)^2]=\lVert\int H_s\diff M_s\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}\;.
\end{equation}
Thus we want to understand \cref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} as the statement that the map 
\begin{align}\label{eq:integral map as isometry}
     I:&H\mapsto \int H\diff M\\
     &\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{H}_0^2\;,
\end{align}
is a linear isometry between \(L\) and \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) for some appropriate normed vector space \(L\). From \cref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes} the natural candidate is the following space:
\begin{definition}\label{def:L^2(M)}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2\). We denote \(L^2(M)\) as the space of predictable processes \(H\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert H\rVert_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{T}H_s^2\diff\langle M\rangle_s]^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}
More generally, we define the general \(L^p(M)\)-space
\begin{definition}\label{def:L^p(M)}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^p\). We denote \(L^p(M)\) as the space of predictable processes \(H\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert H\rVert_{L^p(M)}=\mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^{T}H_s^2\diff[M]_s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}]^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty\;.
    \end{equation}
    for \(p\in[1,\infty)\).
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
 In \cref{def:L^p(M)} we used the process \([M]\) because the process \(\langle M\rangle\) is not guaranteed to exist. Nevertheless, because if it exists, \(\langle M\rangle\) is the unique predictable process such that \([M]-\langle M\rangle\) is a local martingale, the definitions \cref{def:L^p(M)} and \cref{def:L^2(M)} are consistent. For a proof, see\parencite[Thm.~19,Ch.III]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}.
\end{remark}
Hence, \cref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} reads exactly as the statement that the map \(I:L^2(M)\cap\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}^2_0\) given by \(H\mapsto \int H\diff M\) as defined in \cref{eq:integral of simple processes} is an isometry of normed vector spaces. This important structural insight will enable us to extend the stochastic integral to all of \(L^2(M)\). Indeed, we will show that \(\mathcal{E}\) is a dense subset of \(L^2(M)\); hence, we define the stochastic integral of processes in \(L^2(M)\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) as the unique continuous extension of the linear, bounded map \cref{eq:integral map as isometry} for \(L=L^2(M)\).
\bigbreak
Observe that the suggestive notation \(L^p(M)\) as some sort of \(L^p\)-space is justified in the case \(p=2\). Indeed, we define the following measure on the measure product space \(\left(\Omega\times\mathbb{T}_+,\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{T}_+\right)\):
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{P}_M(A)=\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{T}1_A\diff\langle M\rangle_s]\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{T}_+)\;.
\end{equation}
 Then we have, by \cref{def:L^2(M)}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:L^2(M) as Hilbert space}
    L^2(M) = L^2(\Omega\times \mathbb{T}_+,\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\;,
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{P}\) is the sub-\(\sigma\)-algebra of \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{T}_+)\) generated by adapted, left-continuous processes. As usual, \cref{eq:L^2(M) as Hilbert space} is to be understood as the quotient space \(L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)=\mathcal{L}^2(\mathbb{P}_M)/\sim\) with \(X\sim Y\iff \lVert X-Y\rVert_{L^2(M)}=0\), making \(\lVert \cdot\rVert_{L^2(M)}\) a well-defined norm on \(L^2(M)\). In particular, this implies \(L^2(M)\) is a Hilbert space with inner product \(( X,Y)_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{T}X Y\diff\langle M\rangle_s]\). In particular, \(L^2(M)\) is a Banach space. 
\\\\
Consider the following useful lemma, assuring that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is finite.
\begin{lemma}\label{eq:P_M is finite measure}
    Suppose \(M\) is a local martingale starting at \(0\). Then \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) if and only if \(\mathbb{E}[[M]_{T}]<\infty\)
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Under the equivalence of conventions stated in \cref{rem:equivalence of H^p convetions} the proof is given in \parencite[Cor.~4,Ch.II]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}.
\end{proof}
One can show that the process \(\langle M\rangle\) exists if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,loc}^2\) where \(\mathcal{H}_{0,loc}^2\) is the space of processes that are locally in \(\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Observe that the space of locally continuous martingales, denoted \(\mathcal{M}_{0,loc}^c\) is a subset of \(\mathcal{H}_{0,loc}^2\). 
\bigbreak
Now we can prove that the processes in \(L^2(M)\) are approximated by elementary predictable processes. The proof will be based on an application of the functional version of the monotone class theorem \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}, which exploits the fact that 
\begin{equation}
    \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}\;. 
\end{equation}
We are quite detailed and explicit in this part of the chapter, as some arguments in \cref{sec:From simple processes to stochastic integral}.
\\\\
We will actually show that \(\sigma(b\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}\), where \(b\mathcal{E}\) denotes the processes in \(\mathcal{E}\) that are bounded.  The next lemma shows that this is equivalent to the full statement \(\sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}\).
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions}
    Let \(\mathcal{M}\) be a class of real-valued functions on \(\Omega\). We denote by \(b\mathcal{M}\) the class of bounded functions in \(\mathcal{M}\). Then
    \begin{equation}
        \sigma(b\mathcal{M})=\sigma(\mathcal{M})\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The inclusion \(\sigma(b\mathcal{M})\subset\sigma(\mathcal{M})\) is clear. For the converse, let us consider \(X\in\mathcal{M}\) and define
    \begin{equation}
        X^n = -n\vee X\wedge n\in b \mathcal{M}\;.
    \end{equation}
    Then it is clear that \(X^n\to X\) pointwise \footnote{It should be clear that pointwise means: \(X^n(\omega,t)\to X(\omega,t)\quad\forall (\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{T}_+\)} as \(n\to \infty\), hence \(X\) is \(\sigma(b\mathcal{M})\)-measurable as the pointwise limit of processes in \(b\mathcal{M}\), because pointwise limits preserve measurability. Because \(\sigma(\mathcal{M})\) is the smallest \(\sigma\)-algebra such that all \(X\in\mathcal{M}\) are measurable the claim follows.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}
    For \(\mathcal{E}\) as in \cref{def:elementary predictable process} and \(\mathcal{P}\) the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra, we have that 
    \begin{equation}
        \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}\;.
    \end{equation}
    \begin{proof}
        By definition, \(\mathcal{P}\) is generated by the family of adapted, left-continuous processes. Every element of \(\mathcal{E}\) is clearly adapted and left-continuous; hence, \(\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subset\mathcal{P}\) is clear. It remains to show that \(\mathcal{P}\subset\sigma(\mathcal{E})\). For this, we first show that any adapted left-continuous process is the limit of elements of \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). Let \(X\) be an adapted, left-continuous process. By \cref{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions} we might assume \(X\) to be bounded. We define
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous process}
            X^n= X_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{k\geq 1}X_{k/n}1_{(k/n,(k+1)/n]}
        \end{equation}
        As \(X\) is adapted, \(X_{k/n}\) is bounded and \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\)-measurable; hence, indeed \(X^n\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). Then, for every fixed \(t\in\mathbb{T}_+\), there exists a unique \(k(n)\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(\frac{k(n)}{n}<t\leq \frac{k(n)+1}{n}\). Hence, \(X_t^n=X_{k(n)/n}\), thus \(X_t^n\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\subset\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable; hence, adapted. Furthermore, notice that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{k(n)}{n}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}|\frac{k(n)}{n}-t|+t\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}+t=t\) and \(\frac{k(n)}{n}<t\); hence, \(k(n)\nearrow t\). Thus, for every \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{T}_+,\;X^n_t(\omega)\to X_t(\omega)\) as \(n\to\infty\) by the left-continuity of \(X\). This implies 
        \begin{equation}
            \mathcal{P}\subset\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\;.
        \end{equation}
        Indeed, every adapted, left-continuous process \(X\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\) as the point-wise limit of elements in \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). But \(\mathcal{P}\) is the smallest such \(\sigma\)-algebra \parencite[Thm.~7.2.4.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}.
        \\\\
 Furthermore, every \(H\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\) is trivially the pointwise limit of processes
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating approximating-sequence of adapted left-continuous processes}
            H^{m}=H^01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{k=1}^mH^{i}1_{(t_k,t_{k+1}]}\in\mathcal{E}\;,
        \end{equation}
        for \(m\to\infty\).
        By the same argument as before, we have
        \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{P}\subset\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\subset\sigma(\mathcal{E})\;.
        \end{equation}
        \end{proof}
\end{lemma}
Taking a closer look, we see that the previous proof shows the following corollary
\begin{corollary}
    Let \(\mathcal{A}=\left\{A\times(s,t], A\in\mathcal{F}_s, s<t\in\mathbb{T}_+\right\}\cup \left\{\left\{0\right\}\times A, A\in\mathcal{F}_0\right\}\). Then \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{P}\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Every indicator function \(1_A\) with \(A\in\mathcal{A}\) is adapted and left-continuous; hence, it is predictable. The converse inclusion follows from the very same argument as in \cref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}. Indeed, every adapted left-continuous process \(X\) is the limit of processes \(X^n\) of the form \cref{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous process}. Every \(X^n\) is the limit of processes \(X^n_m\) of the form \cref{eq:approximating approximating-sequence of adapted left-continuous processes}. The latter is clearly seen to be \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as \(X_{k/n}\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\)-measurable. Indeed, for \(B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)
    \begin{align}
    (X_{t/k}1_{(t/k,k+1/n]})^{-1}(B)=
    \begin{cases}
        X_{t/k}^{-1}(B)\times (k/n,k+1/n] \text{ if }0\notin B\\
        X_{t/k}^{-1}(B)\times (k/n,k+1/n]\cup \Omega\times \mathbb{R}/(k/n,k+1/n]\text{ if }0\in B
    \end{cases}
    \end{align}
    but \( \Omega\times \mathbb{R}/(k/n,k+1/n]=(\Omega\times(k/n,k+1/n])^c\in\sigma(\mathcal{A})\). In every case \\
    \((X_{t/k}1_{(t/k,k+1/n]})^{-1}(B)\in\sigma(\mathcal{A})\).
 Thus, \(X^n\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as a pointwise limit of measurable processes; hence, so is \(X\).
\end{proof}
We denote \(b\mathcal{P}\) the set of bounded, predictable processes. We can finally state and prove the density of elementary processes in \(L^2(M)\).
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\mathcal{E}\) as defined in \cref{def:elementary predictable process}. Then \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\)\;.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The proof uses essentially the functional version of the monotone class theorem and dominated convergence. By \cref{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions} and \cref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra} we have 
    \begin{equation}
        \sigma(b\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}\;.
    \end{equation}
    Let 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{H}=\left\{ H\in b\mathcal{P}:H\in L^2(M)\;,\forall \epsilon>0\;, \exists H_{\epsilon}\in b\mathcal{E}\text{ with }\lVert H_{\epsilon}-H\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon\right\}\;.
    \end{equation}
    Suppose we were able to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) was a monotone class containing the algebra of functions \(b\mathcal{E}\). We would conclude by the monotone class theorem \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded \(\sigma(b\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}\)-measurable maps, which would show that \(b\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\). To get density in \(L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) we observe that for \(H\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) we can define 
    \begin{equation}
        H^n=-n\vee H\wedge n\in bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)
    \end{equation}
    with \(H^n\mapsto H\) pointwise. In particular \(|H^n|\leq|H|\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M),\;n\in\mathbb{N}\), hence by dominated convergence 
    \begin{equation}
        H^n\overset{L^2}{\to}H\;.
    \end{equation}
    This shows that \(bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) is dense in \(L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\).
    \\\\
 It remains to show that the application of the monotone class theorem is justified. 
    \\\\
    First, by \cref{eq:P_M is finite measure}, \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is a finite measure; hence, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains \(b\mathcal{E}\), and the constant functions. Indeed for \(X=c\in\mathbb{R}\) take \(X^n=c1_{\left\{0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\). Then \(X^n\in b\mathcal{E}\) and \(X^n\to X\) pointwise and hence, due to dominated convergence, in \(L^2(M)\). Clearly, \(\mathcal{H}\) forms a vector space of functions. Indeed, if \(H_1,H_2\in\mathcal{H}\), for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exist \(H^1_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}},H^2_{\frac{\epsilon}{2|\alpha|}}\) such that, for all \(\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\):
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert H^1+\alpha H^2- H^1_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}-\alpha H^2_{\frac{\epsilon}{2|\alpha|}} \rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq \epsilon\;,
    \end{equation}
    due to the triangle inequality.
 It remains to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) is closed under bounded monotone limits. Hence let \(0<H_0<H_1<\dots\) with \(H_j\in\mathcal{H}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}H_n = H\) pointwise, with \(H\) bounded. Fix \(\epsilon>0\). Then due to dominated convergence 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert H-H_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}=0\;.
    \end{equation}
    Hence, there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that \(\lVert H-H_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon/2\). Now \(H_n\in\mathcal{H}\), hence there exists \(H^n_{\epsilon/2}\in b\mathcal{E}\) such that \(\lVert H_n-H^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon/2\). By the triangle inequality we have
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert H-H^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert H-H_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert H_n-H^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon
    \end{equation}
 Hence, we conclude by the monotone class theorem. 
\end{proof}
For the general case \(p\in[1,\infty)\) the proof goes through similarly after a couple of observations. Arguing about \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) implying that \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is a finite measure is non-sensical in the general case, as \(L^p(M)\) is not an \(L^p\) on \(\Omega\times\mathbb{T}_+\) for some measure \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_M\). Nevertheless, due to Burkholder-Davis-Gundi (BDG) \parencite[Thm.~48,Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, we have for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\) that there exists \(c_p>0\) such that 
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}[[X]^{\frac{p}{2}}]\leq \frac{1}{c_p}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}|M|^p]=\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}<\infty\;.
\end{equation}
Hence, we have for the same truncation argument that 
\(X^n=c1_{\left\{ 0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\) converges pointwise to \(X\in L^p(M)\), and thus
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^T (H^n_s-c)^2\diff [M]_s]\right)^{p/2}]\leq |c|^p\mathbb{E}[[M]^{\frac{p}{2}}]<\infty\;.
\end{equation}
hence \(\mathcal{H}\) containes again the constant functions. The truncation argument works by two successive applications of dominated convergence, showing that \(V^n=-n\vee V\wedge n\to V\) in \(L^p(M)\). The stability under bounded monotone limits follows similarly. Hence, we get the following proposition:
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) and \(\mathcal{E}\) as defined in \cref{def:elementary predictable process}. Then \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^p(M)\)\;.
\end{proposition}
For the details of the proof, we refer to the appendix \cref{app:proof of E density in L^p(M) via monotone class}.
\bigbreak
Finally, having proven that the elementary processes are dense in \(L^2(M)\), we can use the isometry property for the map \cref{eq:integral map as isometry} to extend the integral to a bounded linear operator by the standard extension theorem \parencite[Prop.~5.4]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}. This uses, in particular, that \(L^2(M)\) and \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) are Banach spaces, which follows from the fact that they can both be identified with an \(L^2\) space. Hence, as Hilbert spaces, they are in particular Banach.
We get the following theorem.
\begin{theorem}\label{def:square integrable stochastic integral}
    The linear map \(H\mapsto \int H\diff M\) of \(\mathcal{E}\) into \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) extends uniquely to an isometric linear map \(L^2(M)\mapsto\mathcal{H}_0^2\). We call this extension the integral of \(H\) with respect to \(M\).
\end{theorem}
Notice that as we define the stochastic integral as the extension of a map \(I:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}_0^2\) to \(L^2(M)\) where \(\mathcal{E}\subset L^2(M)\) is a dense subset, the resulting integral is in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) by construction. 
\\\\
Furthermore, the isometry property is generally know as Itô's isometry. According to \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}, it even holds when \(M\) is only locally in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\begin{theorem}[Itô Isometry]\label{thm:Ito Isometry}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{loc}\), then for \(H\in L^2(M)\)
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert H\rVert_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}^2[\int_0^{T}H_s^2\diff \langle M\rangle_s]=\mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^{T}H_s\diff M_s\right)^2]=\lVert \int H_s\diff M_s\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}^2
    \end{equation}
\end{theorem}
We will refer to the setting in \cref{def:square integrable stochastic integral} as the \textit{square integrable setting}. From here, one can start and broaden the space of integrands and integrators. We give a useful characterization of the integral in \cref{def:square integrable stochastic integral}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and \(H\in L^2(M)\). The stochastic integral \(H\bullet M\) is the unique process in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) such that, for every \(N\in\mathcal{H}^2\),
    \begin{equation}
        \langle M\bullet N,N\rangle=\int H\diff\langle M,N\rangle\;,\forall N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2
    \end{equation}
\end{proposition}
For the proof, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. More precisely, \parencite{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus} shows that everything presented in the square-integrable setting up to now remains valid if we take \(M\) to be only locally in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). For this, see \parencite[sec.~4.2.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. 
\\\\
Conceptually, note that the right-hand side is a linear form in \(N\) that is valid on \(\mathcal{H}^2\), which has been identified with a Hilbert space. By the Kunita-Watanabe inequality \parencite[Thm.11.4.1]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} it is also continuous. Then \(L\) is the unique Riesz-Fischer vector in \(\mathcal{H}^2\) such that the continuous linear form can be written as an inner product. The latter characterizes the continuous linear form on a Hilbert space uniquely. 
One can further extend the integral of \cref{def:square integrable stochastic integral} to integrators in \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,loc}\) and integrands in \(H\in L^2_{loc}(M)\), where the latter is defined to be the class of predictable processes \(H\) such that there exists a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) for which \(H1_{(0,\tau_n]}\) is in \(L^2(M)\). 
We give the definition of \(L_{loc}^2(M)\).
\begin{definition}\label{def:L^2(M)_loc}
 For \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,loc}\), the space \(L_{loc}^2(M)\) consists of predictable processes \(H\) for which there is  a sequence of stopping times \(\tau_n\nearrow\infty\) almost surely, such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\tau_n}H_s^2\diff \langle M\rangle_s]<\infty\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\;.
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}
In particular, we have that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,loc}^2\), \(H\in L^2_{loc}(M)\) is equivalent to \(H1_{(0,\tau_n]}\in L^2(M)\) for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), and is equivalent to \(H\in L^2(M^{\tau_n})\) for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).
\bigbreak
The extension of \cref{def:square integrable stochastic integral} for the localized objects is then done by setting 
\begin{equation}\label{def:local stochastic integral}
    I_M(H)^{(k)}\coloneqq H1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet X^{\tau_k}\quad \text{ on } (0,\tau_k] \text{ and } I_M(H)_0=0 \;,
\end{equation}
where \((\tau_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a common localizing sequence of \(H\) and \(M\). To understand that the objects are well-defined we recall a well-known property of the integral defined in \cref{def:square integrable stochastic integral}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\), \(\tau\) be a stopping time and \(H\in L^2(M)\). Then
\begin{equation}
    (H\bullet M)^{\tau}=H\bullet(M)^{\tau}=H1_{(0,\tau]}\bullet M\;.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
Hence 
\begin{equation}
    H1_{(0,\tau_k]}\in L^2(M^{\tau_k})\;,
\end{equation} 
so the integral on the right-hand side of \cref{def:local stochastic integral} is well-defined. It remains to check that the definition defines the integral on all of \(\Omega\times\mathbb{T}_+\) and that it does so consistently; that is, \(I_M(H)^{(k)}_t=I_M(H)^{(k-1)}_t\) for \(t<\tau_{k-1}\). For the first part, this follows from the definition of a localizing sequence as \(\tau_k\nearrow\infty\). The latter follows from the fact that 
\begin{equation}
(I_M(H)^{(k)}_t)^{\tau_{k-1}}=I_M(H)^{(k-1)}_t\,.
\end{equation}
 The integral can then be defined by "gluing" the pieces together 
\begin{align}
    I_M(H)_t(\omega)=
    \begin{cases}
        \sum_{k\geq 1}I_M(H)^{(k)}_t(\omega)1_{(\tau_k(\omega)<t\leq\tau_{k+1}(\omega)]}\text{ for } t\in\mathbb{T}_+/\left\{0\right\}\\
        0 \text{ for } t=0
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
For details and a different point of view, we refer to \parencite[Prop.~2.20]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. In particular, there exists a characterization of this localized integral analogous to \cref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}. For details, see \parencite[Prop.~2.20]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. In the case \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,loc}^2\), we thus set the class of integrable processes by 
\begin{equation}
  L(M)=L_{loc}^2(M)\;,
\end{equation}
where \(L^2_{loc}(M)\) as in \cref{def:L^2(M)_loc}. Notice that in this stochastic integration, the local objects are themselves only a local martingale. 
\begin{remark}\label{rem:local integrator global integrand yields global integral}
Notice that the stochastic integral for the local objects, as defined in \cref{def:local stochastic integral} is itself only a locally square integrable local martingale. 
   For \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,loc}^2\) and \(M\in L^2(M)\) the respective stochastic integral is still in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. 
\end{remark}
\subsection{Kunita-Watanabe Decomposition}\label{sec:Kunita-Watanabe Decomposition}
Before turning towards stochastic integration with respect to classical semimartingales, we profit from the current familiarity of the reader with the square integrable setup to introduce and state the celebrated Kunita Watanabe decomposition, which shall play a central role in the solution theory for the mean-variance hedging problem for square integrable martingale price processes. For this, we will need a stronger notion of orthogonality on \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) than the one induced by the inner product structure. The fact that \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) is a space of uniformly integrable martingales leads to a richer geometry than the Hilbert space structure discussed in \cref{eq:H^p identification}. 
\begin{definition}
    Let \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). We say that \(M\) is strongly orthogonal to \(N\) and vise versa if their product \(MN\) is a martingale. In this case, we denote \(M\perp N\).
\end{definition}
In particular, for \(M\perp N\),
\begin{equation}
    (M,N)_{\mathcal{H}_0^2}=\mathbb{E}[M_{T}N_{T}]=\mathbb{E}[M_0N_0]=0\;.
\end{equation}
Hence, strong orthogonality implies the weak orthogonality of \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
Furthermore, we have that \(M\perp N\iff \langle M,N\rangle=0\) \parencite[Rem.~11.2.5.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}.
\bigbreak
As explained in \cref{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}, in the context of mean-variance hedging, given a fixed price process \(X\), we are especially interested in studying the strategies for which the gains process evaluated at the final time is again square integrable. In the square integrable setting, we provided a partial answer affirming that for \(X\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), strategies in \(L^2(M)\) fulfill this requirement. More precisely, we are interested in such strategies as they allow us to consider the mean-variance hedging results \cref{eq:mean squared hedging} as a projection problem of the liability \(H\) onto the \(L^2\) subspace \(\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\), at least onto its closure if it is not closed itself. Alternatively, we can see this from a different angle. If the liability \(H\) were attainable, that is, if there existed \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) such that
\begin{equation}
    H=\int_0^T\vartheta_s\diff X_s\;.
\end{equation}
This is a statement about the representability of \(H\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\) as a stochastic integral with respect to \(X\). By assumption, we are in an incomplete market; hence, such a representation property can't be true in general. Nevertheless, we may ask whether we can separate the part of \(H\) that is representable by an integral with respect to \(X\) from the part that is not. The integrable part should be well distinguishable from the non-integral part. It is one of the fundamental insights of linear spaces that this is best done by decomposition into orthogonal parts. Obviously, this amounts to the same projection problem as previously discussed. In the square integrable setting, the geometry of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) rich enough that we can provide complete theoretical answers to this question. The fundamental property is that the space of stochastic integrals with respect to square integrable processes is closed in \(L^2\). This will give rise to the celebrated Kunita-Watanabe decomposition, which provides the representation theoretic solution to mean-square hedging for square integrable martingale price processes. We need the following notion.
\begin{definition}\label{def:stable subspace}
    A \textit{stable} subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) is a closed linear subspace \(\mathcal{A}\) of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) which is stable under stopping: For every \(M\in\mathcal{A}\) and every stopping time \(\tau\), the stopped process \(M^{\tau}\) is in \(\mathcal{A}\).
\end{definition}
The following example of a stable subspace constitutes the strong structural property of stochastic integrals in the square integrable setting
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:stochastic integrals as stable subspace}
    For every \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) the space 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{I}^2(M)=\left\{\int H\diff M:H\in L^2(M)\right\}
    \end{equation}
    is a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The closedness of \( \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) follows directly from \cref{thm:Ito Isometry} as \( \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is the isometric image under the map \(H\to H\bullet M\) of the Hilbert space \(L^2(M)\). Stability under stopping follows from \cref{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals}. Indeed, let \(\tau\) be a stopping time and \(N=H\bullet M\), the \(N^{\tau}=H1_{(0,\tau]}\bullet M\). As \(H\in L^2(M)\) it follows that \(H1_{(0,\tau]}\in L^2(M)\). Hence, \(N^{\tau}\in \mathcal{I}^2(M)\); thus, \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is stable under stopping. 
\end{proof}
Furthermore, we have that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closedness of vartheta bullet M_T in L^2}
     G_{T}(L^2(M))=\left\{\int_0^{T} H\diff M:H\in L^2(M)\right\}
\end{equation}
is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{T})\). Indeed, notice that under the identification discussed in \cref{eq:H^p identification}
\begin{align*}
    &H\mapsto H_T\\
    &\mathcal{H}^2\to L^2(\mathcal{F}_{T})\;.
\end{align*} 
\cref{thm:Ito Isometry} reads as an isometry of Hilbert-spaces \(L^2(M)\to L^2(\mathcal{F}_{T})\). In particular, \(G_{T}(L^2(M))\) is the isometric image of the complete space \(L^2(M)\) in \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_T)\). The following shows the importance of a stable subspace in understanding the geometry of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:weak and strong stable subspaces}
    Suppose \(\mathcal{A}\) is a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and denote by 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{A}^{\Delta}=\left\{N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2:(N,M)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=0\quad\forall M\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
    \end{equation}
    the weakly orthogonal complement of \(\mathcal{A}\) in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). Then \(\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}\) is also strongly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{A}\) and a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\end{proposition}
For the proof, we refer to \parencite[Prop.~5.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}
Note that the latter is conceptually astonishing. Weak orthogonality of two processes only states \(L^2\) orthogonality of their terminal values. It says nothing about the relation between the processes overall. Nevertheless, the weak orthogonal complement of stable subspaces is, in particular, strongly orthogonal, which is a property of processes. 
\begin{theorem}[Kunita-Watanabe Decomposition]\label{thm:KW}
    Fix \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Then every \(N\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) can be uniquely written as 
    \begin{equation}
        N = \int H\diff M +L
    \end{equation}
    for some \(H\in L^2(M)\) and some \(L\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) with \(L\perp M\). Furthermore, \(H=\frac{\diff \langle M,N\rangle}{\diff \langle M\rangle}\) is a predictable density of \(\langle M,N\rangle\) with respect to \(\langle M\rangle\). 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    Assume that \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). Let \(\hat{N}\) be the weakly orthogonal projection in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) onto the closed subspace \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\). Hence, there exists \(H\in L^2(M)\) such that \(N=\int H\diff M\). Consider
    \begin{equation}
        L\coloneqq N-\hat{N}\;.
    \end{equation}
    \(L\) is weakly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) by construction.
