We will not further investigate this route as we independantly require the closedness of \(G_T(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\), which is a sufficient conditions as just seen. The closedeness of \(G_T(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\) is completely characterised by \parencite[Thm.~4.1]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM} under the requirement that \(X\) is continuous semimartingale. 
\bigbreak
The key additional ingredient, as far as we are concerned in this article, is the reversed Hölder inequality. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:reverse Hölder}
If $L$ is a uniformly integrable martingale such that $L_0=1$ and $L_T>0$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s., then we say that
$L$ \emph{satisfies the reverse H\"older inequality under $\mathbb{P}$}, denoted by $R_p(\mathbb{P})$, where
$1<p\le +\infty$, if and only if there is a constant $C$ such that for every $t$, we have
\[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(\frac{L_T}{L_t}\right)^p \,\middle|\, \mathcal{F}_t\right]\le C.
\]
For \(p=+\infty\) we require that \(\frac{L_T}{L_t}\) is bounded by \(C\). 
\end{definition}
We say that an ELMM \(\mathbb{Q}\) satisfies \(\mathcal{R}_p(\mathbb{P})\) if the respective density process 
\begin{equation}
    Z_t=\mathbb{E}[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}|\mathcal{F}_t]
\end{equation}
satisfies \(\mathcal{R}_p(\mathbb{P})\).\\\\

The relevant part of  \parencite[Thm.4.1]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM} goes as follows:
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)}
Let $X$ denote a continuous semimartingale. Then the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is an equivalent local martingale measure with square integrable density and
      $G_T(\Theta)$ is closed in $L^2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item There is a square integrable local martingale measure $\mathbb{Q}$ that satisfies the inequality
      $\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item The variance optimal measure \(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\) is in \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) and satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
For a proof, discussion, and the complete statement, we refer to \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}.
The third equivalent statement in \cref{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)} links the general discussion of mean-variance hedging in \cref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}.

Thus, to have an existence claim about the mean-variance hedging solution, we shall require \(X\) to be continuous. We can finally state an extension of 
\cref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to price processes in \(L^2(M,A)\). Recall the notation \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) for the set of equivalent local martingale measure \(\mathbb{Q}\sim \mathbb{P}\) such that \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\).
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}
    Assume the \(X=X_0+M+A\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}_{loc}^2\) with \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\). Assume that there exists \(\epsilon>0\) for which \(M\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\epsilon}\). Furthermore, assume that some \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\) satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\) inequality. Let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{T}\)-measurable random variable. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale setup, continuous deep variance hedging}
    \min_{c\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(H-c-\int_{0}^T\vartheta_t\diff X_t\bigr)^2\Bigr]
    \,=\,\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in \mathcal{E}(\psi)}
    \mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(H-c-\int_{0}^T\vartheta_t\diff X_t\bigr)^2\Bigr]\;,
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
   The assumption imply by \cref{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)} that \(G_T(\Theta)\) is closed in  \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Hence the minimum on the left-hand side exist by the Hilbert projection Theorem. Furthermore, according to \cref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)} the conditions imply that \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds, hence \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\).
\end{proof}
The previous statement only involves \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). Hence, 
according to \cref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi) under compacteness}, considering the market information process \(\widetilde{Y}=\tanh(Y)\) instead of \(Y\), effectively replacing \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) by \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) we can let go of the additional \(\epsilon\)-integrability condition on \(M\). We get the following version. 


\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}
    Assume the \(X=X_0+M+A\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}_{loc}^2\), with \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\). Furthermore, assume that some \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\) satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\) inequality. Let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{T}\)-measurable random variable. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale setup, continuous deep variance hedging}
    \min_{c\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(H-c-\int_{0}^T\vartheta_t\diff X_t\bigr)^2\Bigr]
    \,=\,\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in \widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)}
    \mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(H-c-\int_{0}^T\vartheta_t\diff X_t\bigr)^2\Bigr]\;,
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
The proof is the same as for \cref{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness} using the density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\) in \(L^2(M)\) by \cref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}.

\chapter{Conclusion}
\section{Summary of contributions}
This thesis studied theoretical approximation aspects of neural networks in continuous-time mathematical finance, with a particular focus on mean--variance hedging. The starting point was the classical hedging problem of approximating a contingent claim \(H\) by terminal values of self-financing portfolios. In particular we studied in depth the mean-variance hedging optimization problem 
\begin{equation}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Theta}\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-\int_0^TH\diff X\right)^2]<\infty\;.
\end{equation}
We studied this problem for different levels of generality of \(X\).In the square-integrable setting, the problem reduced to a Hilbert-projection problem and the solution was explicitely characterized by the Kunita-Watanabe decomposition of \(H\) with respect to \(X\). In the more gerenal semimartingale setting, we had to study the closedness of 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{A}(\Theta)=\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\;,
\end{equation}
in more general terms for different choices of \(\Theta\). 
\bigbreak
We espescially focused on how to approximate the optimal mean-variance solution by leveraging density properties of neural networks. In the finite sample space, discrete-time deep hedging framework we discussed in depth the theoritcal approach proposed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. For the general case we followed \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}, understood why approach is particularly convenient and how it can be used to formulate and proof a deep-mean variance hedging result in the continouous time, square integrable setting. In particular we showed how to improve on the integrability conditions of \parencite[Thm.~5.32]{Arandjelovic2025} by a change of coordinates for the market information process. 

Finally we showed how to generalize this continuous time deep mean variance hedging result to the semimartingale case, in particular by leveraging the density arguments \parencite[Prop.~5.28.]{Arandjelovic2025} to the space
\begin{equation}
    \Theta=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\;.
\end{equation}

Overall, the thesis shows that, if done properly, the approach developped in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} is extremly powerful in particular in the treatement of the mean-variance hedging problem. Neural networks are used as dense classes of deterministic functions from which predictable trading strategies are generated. The key mathematical task is to identify the correct function spaces and continuity properties that allow deterministic approximation results to pass through stochastic integration and ultimately to the mean-variance hedging problem.

The results obtained in this thesis are purely theoretical. Numerical implementation, training procedures, convergence rates, and empirical performance were not considered. These aspects would form natural directions for further work. Another possible direction is to study more explicitly how restrictive the assumptions in the semimartingale extension are in concrete financial models. Finally, it would be interesting to investigate whether similar approximation ideas can be developed for other hedging criteria or for models including additional market frictions.

\begin{appendices}

\section{Appendix:Infima over closure}\label{Appendix:Infima over closure}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:minimization of closure}
     Let X be a normed space, \(Y\subset X\) a subset and \(d:X\to\mathbb{R}\) continous. Then 
     \begin{equation}
         \inf d(Y)=\inf d(\overline{Y})\;.
     \end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Because \(Y\subset\overline{Y}\) we have \(\inf d(\overline{Y})\leq\inf d(Y)\). For the other direction we need continuity of \(d:X\to\mathbb{R}\). Let \(y\in\overline{Y}\). There exists \((y_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset Y\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}y_n = y\) in \(X\). By continuity of \(d\) we thus have \(\lim_{n\to\infty}d(y_n) = d(y)\). Furthermore, by definition,
    \begin{equation}
        d(y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}d(y_n)\geq\inf d(Y)\;.
    \end{equation}
    Taking the infimum over \(\overline{Y}\) on both sides we have,
    \begin{equation}
        \inf d(\overline{Y}) \geq \inf d(Y);.
    \end{equation}
\end{proof}
Notice that the above proof also works if \(d\) is only upper-semi continuous. Then we have \(d(y)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}{d(y_n)}\) and we get the same conclusion. Furthermore, we can generalize the above to a general topological space \(Y\) by replacing sequences by nets, as all we have used is that for \(y\in\overline{Y}\) there exists \((y_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset Y\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}y_n = y\) in \(X\)
\section{Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}\label{Appendix:Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}
\begin{proof}
    Recall that 
    \begin{align}
        &\Theta=\left\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=0}^{n-1}:\vartheta_k \text{ is }\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\text{-measurable}\right\}\\
        &\Theta_M\left\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=0}^{n-1}:\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1}),F_k\in\Psi_M\right\}\\
        &\Theta_{\infty}=\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Theta_M\;.
    \end{align}
    By the Doob-representation Lemma we have for \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) that there exists \(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{rk}\to\mathbb{R}\),  (Borel-)measurable such that
    \begin{equation}
        \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1})\;.
    \end{equation}
    Because \(\Omega\) is finit we have that \(f_k\in L^2(\mathbb{R}^{rk},\mu)\), where \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1})\). Hence, we see that 
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\;.
    \end{equation}
    Furthermore let
    \begin{equation}
        \Xi_k=\left\{X:\mathcal{L}^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}):X=F_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\text{ where } F_k\in\Psi\right\}
    \end{equation}
    Then,
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta_{\infty}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_k\;.
    \end{equation}
    By density of of \(\Psi\) in \(L^2\) we have \(\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}=L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_k,\mathbb{P})\).
\end{proof}
We define the following norm on \(\Theta\):
\begin{equation}
    \lVert(\vartheta_1,\dots,\vartheta_{n})\rVert\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lVert \vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}
\end{equation}
Then we have that 
\begin{equation}
    \overline{\Theta}_{\infty}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_k,\mathbb{P})=\Theta\;,\footnotemark
\end{equation}\footnotetext{This can be checked by hand. The inclusion \(\overline{\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_k}\subset\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\) follows from the fact that \(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\) is closed w.r.t. to the product topology which is exactly induced by the norm \(\lVert(\vartheta_1,\dots,\vartheta_{n})\rVert\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lVert \vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}\). Recalling that \(\overline{\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_k}\)is by definition the smallest closed set containing \(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_k\), the inclusion follows. The reverse inclusion can be checked by hand.}
in particular \(\overline{\Theta}_{\infty}=\overline{\Theta}\), where the closures are taken with respect to the previously introduced norm.
If we show that the map 
\begin{equation}
    J:\vartheta\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\left(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)^2]
\end{equation}
is continuous with  to the \(\lVert\cdot\rVert\)-induced topology, the 
\cref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging} follows from \cref{prop:minimization of closure}. The continuity follows from the repetitive application of Cauchy-Schwarz and the observation that in the finite case 
\begin{equation}
    (\vartheta\bullet X)_T=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\vartheta_k(X_{k}-X_{k-1})\leq2\max_{0\leq k\leq n}\max_{\omega\in\Omega}|X_k(\omega)|\sum_{k=1}^n\vartheta_k\;,
\end{equation}
where \(C(X)\coloneqq\max_{0\leq k\leq n}\max_{\omega\in\Omega}|X_k(\omega)|<\infty\) as this \(X_k\) is a finite random variable on the finite sample space \(\Omega\).
Thus, the map \(J\) is continuous as the composition of two continuous maps. 

\section{Proof of density of \(\mathcal{E}\) in \(L^p(M)\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(p\geq 1\)}\label{app:proof of E density in L^p(M) via monotone class}
Let 
\begin{equation}
        \mathcal{H}=\left\{ H\in b\mathcal{P}:H\in L^p(M)\;,\forall \epsilon>0\;, \exists H_{\epsilon}\in b\mathcal{E}\text{ with }\lVert H_{\epsilon}-H\rVert_{L^p(M)}<\epsilon\right\}\;.
    \end{equation}
The structure of the proof and the main arguments are the same as in \cref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}, hence we refer to the latter for the structure of the proof. We simply show how to generalize the three truncation arguments apprearing in \cref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}. In order to argue why it is enough to show that \(b\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(bL^p(M)\) we take \(H^n=-n\vee H\wedge n\) which converges pointwise to \(H\in L^p(M)\). We argue why the convergence is also in the \(L^p(M)\) sense. By dominated convergence
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_0^T(H_s(\omega)^n-H_s(\omega))^2\diff [M]_s(\omega)=0\;,\forall \omega\in\Omega
    \end{equation}
    Due to \(|H^n-H|\leq|H|\) and therefore
    \begin{equation}
        \int_0^T|H^n-H|_s^2\diff[M]_s\leq\int_0^T|H|_s^2\diff[M]_s\;.
    \end{equation}
    Thus again by dominated convergence
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^T|H_s^n-H_s|\diff [M]_s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}]=0\;.
    \end{equation}
 Furthermore, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains the constant functions because for \(X^n=1_{\left\{0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\), which converges pointwise to \(X=c\), it does so too in the \(L^p(M)\) sense. Indeed, by the same argument as before, first using dominated convergence pathwise and then again to conclude using 
    \begin{equation}
        \left(\int_0^T |X^n-X|^2\diff[M]_s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\leq |c|^p[M]^{\frac{p}{2}}\;, 
    \end{equation}
    where the right-hand side is integrable, as previously established.
    The stability under bounded monotone limits follows analogously as for the case \(H^n=-n\vee H\wedge n\). Indeed, the convergence is monotone and bounded, so we have \(H\leq C\) and for a monotone limit \(\widetilde{H}^n\)
    \begin{equation}
        |\widetilde{H}^n-H|\leq2C\;.
    \end{equation}
    Thus, the same successive application of dominated convergence applies.
    The rest is completely analogous to \cref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}. This completes the proof.

\section{With loss of generality argument }\label{Appendix:w.l.o.g.}
We provide some details on why we can assume \(\theta\bullet A\) to be increasing w.l.o.g. For this, we need the following result from \parencite{AnselStricker1992LoisMartingale}, in particular \parencite[Thm.~7]{AnselStricker1992LoisMartingale}. 
\begin{theorem}
Let \(D\) be an increasing, predictable, right-continuous process, and let
\(A\) be a predictable, right-continuous process of finite variation. Let
\(p\in(1,\infty)\) and let \(q\) be its conjugate exponent, i.e. \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1.\)
If the set
\[
    \mathcal{P}(A,D)=\left\{
        \int_0^1K_s\diff A_s :
        K \text{ predictable and bounded, and }
        \int_0^1 |K_s|^p\,dD_s \leq 1
    \right\}
\]
is bounded in \(L^0\), then there exists a predictable process \(\alpha\)
such that
\[
    \diff A_s = \alpha_s\diff D_s
\]
and
\[
    \int_0^1 |\alpha_s|^q\,dD_s < \infty.
\]
\end{theorem}
We check that we can apply this theorem to with \(D=|A|\). By assumption \(A\) is continuous hence \(|A|\) continuous as well and hence preditable, as a left-continuous adapted process. Clearly \(|A|\) is increasing. Then due to Hölder applied pathwise
\begin{equation}
    \int_0^1|K_s|\diff |A|_s\leq \left(\int_0^1|K_s|^p\diff |A|_s\right)^{1/p}|A|_1^{1/q}\leq|A|_1^{1/q}\;,
\end{equation}
as \(\int_0^1|K_s|^p\diff |A|_s\leq 1\) by assumption. But \(A\) has finite variation hence
\(\forall \epsilon>0\) there exists an \(M>0\) such that
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{P}(|A|_1^{1/q}>M)<\epsilon\;.
\end{equation}
Hence \(\mathcal{P}(A,|A|)\) is bounded in \(L^0\) and thus there exists a predictable density \(\alpha_s\).\\\\
Take the finite variation process 
\begin{equation}
    B_t\coloneqq\int_0^t \vartheta_s\diff A_s \;.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the later is a finite variation process for any finite variation process \(A\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(A)\). By the above, there exists a predictable density \(\beta\) such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:predictable density for total variation}
    \diff B_t=\beta_t\diff |B|_t
\end{equation}
It follows from \cref{eq:predictable density for total variation} that \(\beta_s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}\) for any \(s\in\mathbb{T}_+\). Hence, in \cref{...} if \(\int \vartheta\diff A\) is not monotone the conclusion of the lemma still holds for 
\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{\epsilon}=\epsilon\cdot\beta
\end{equation}
Indeed the latter is a predictable process with values in \(\left\{-1,1\right\}\). Apply the same reasoning in \cref{lemaa} to the increasing, finite variation process \(|B|_t=\int_0^t 1\diff|B|_s\). We get the existence of predictable process \(\widetilde{\epsilon}\) such that for every \(\eta>0\) we have 
\begin{equation}
    \forall t\in[0,T],\quad\left|\int_0^t\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\diff|B|_s\right|\leq\eta
\end{equation}
The integral above can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}
    \int_0^t\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\beta_s\diff B_s=\int_0^t\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\beta_s\vartheta_s\diff A_s\;.
\end{equation}
The claim follows by setting 
\begin{equation}
    \epsilon_s=\widetilde{\epsilon_s}\beta_s\;,
\end{equation}
which defines a predictable process with values in \(\left\{-1,1\right\}\), hence the claim.
\end{appendices}
